#8281. Piolet d'Or Team Shares Insider Tips for Climbing in Peru's Huayhuash Range

Marc Toralles, Ruben Sanmartin, and Bru Busom of Spain were awarded a Piolet d’Or for a highly committing new route on Yerupaja Grande (6,634m), the highest peak of Peru’s Huayhuash range. ExplorersWeb asked the young climbers about the ascent and the unique character of the Andes. They offered some tips for climbers looking to add Peru to their list of high-altitude destinations.

In the last few years, the Andes have reportedly been affected by climate change. “The main effect is that the huge snow structures on the summits are exposed to warmer temperatures and become more fragile,” Sanmartin said. “Basically, they fall down more easily.”

“That means that climbers need to pick their route carefully [when judging] what lingers above them,” Buson explained.

Bru Buson, left, and Marc Toralles. Photo: Ruben Sanmartin

This was especially noticeable last year on Siula Grande. “There was a snowfield midway up the face as we trekked by, and on our way back, it was not there anymore,” said Marc Toralles. “Somehow, the melting process in the Andes, with its 5,000m and even 6,000m [altitudes], is similar to what we are seeing every year in the Alps, where we all work as mountain guides.”

For Toralles, the best thing in the Andes is how accessible the mountains are. “It’s so easy to go climbing there,” he said.

There are no lifts or paved roads, but you can go, climb a peak, and return in three or four days. Something like that in the Himalaya would take weeks. All you need is to get to Huaraz by plane, bus, or car from Lima, where you may stay some days to acclimatize (there are many options), and then go to the mountains.

If your planned peak is not in the right conditions, you will surely find some other one to climb. Also, the options are infinite for ice climbing, alpine climbing, rock, trekking, anything.

The team’s base camp. Photo: Spanish Yerupaja Grande team

Red tape is minimal. There are no permits for peaks. Instead, climbers and trekkers pay a small fee to cross the comunidades (the areas) where they want to climb, and climbers can hire arrieros (riders with mules) to help carry equipment and supplies to base camps or higher. On this occasion, the climbers kept their logistics basic, with little beyond a slightly larger base camp tent.

With some of the more accessible places, it is advisable to have someone watching over your base camp while you are away climbing.

Once you leave the beaten track, you’ll be alone. “There are no crowds in Huayhuash,” Buson said. Why aren’t they more popular? The climbers explained that Huayhuash peaks are typically more difficult than popular options in the Cordillera Blanca, also in Peru. But they note this lack of popularity is changing fast. “We are seeing much more people in recent years, both climbing and trekking,” they said.

Thanks to the many possibilities, the three Spanish climbers didn’t need to do much planning. “We know Huayhuash pretty well, we are familiar with the terrain and the climbing style, and we are aware there are always options,” said Toralles, who has climbed there four times.

That said, the line the Spaniards climbed on Yerupaja last year was not improvised. They had that route in their mind for some time. “We heard of someone who had tried but called the attempt off, so the route was there to be climbed,” Toralles said. “In addition, it didn’t require a long time, and the timing worked for me, since I was traveling to Peru anyway to do some guiding.”

Buson and Sanmartin joined immediately. To acclimatize, they had worked double shifts the previous weeks around Chamonix, where both are professional guides. “Working on the Mont Blanc massif intensively was great training and acclimatization,” they said. “We were ready by the time we set foot on the mountain.”

The best time to climb in Huayhuash is July, August, and the first half of September.

Once on the mountain, they carefully checked the conditions of the face and the line they would follow. “In the Andes, you have to assume that the summit ridge snow formations, the famous ‘mushrooms,’ the big cornices, and the seracs on the sides of the face are mostly in a delicate state. You need to assume that you will progress on deep, unstable snow, and the entire structure may have deteriorated and be prone to crumble,” Buson noted.

To deal with them, the climbers used some custom-made tools: They attached alitas (little wings) to their ice axes for a better grip on foamy, loose snow. They also carried small snowshoes for some sections.

Ice axe with ‘alitas’ (wings). Photo: Nick Sheldon/Mountain Project

Conditions in the Andean mountains are always variable. “You’ll find different terrain depending on the altitude and the orientation,” Sanmartin added. “This is why technical routes on these peaks require a high level of commitment, which, as happened on our route, may be even higher than the intrinsic difficulty of the pitches,” Toralles explained.

With this in mind, the Spanish team named the route they opened Essence of Commitment.

Marc Toralles ready for the ridge, with large snow pickets and an ice axe with ‘wings’ attached. Photo: Buson/Sanmartin

In this sort of terrain, it is paramount to stay focused and make the right call between subjective and objective factors. “There are countless reasons to turn around on climbs like this, but one must keep going, except for obvious reasons, such as an injury, a completely impassable section, or a really violent storm,” Sanmartin said.

“That is why it is important to consider all options and potential issues before starting the climb. You need to be sure you like the line and want to climb it.”

Asked whether they reached a point of no return during their climb, Buson admitted they did. “On reaching the ridge, we became aware that trying to downclimb the same route would be quite complex. However, we also saw the route upward was doable.”

The Spaniards did not count on a professional meteorologist. “We had our friends texting us the online, free forecasts over InReach,” they said. “One of the positive aspects of Huayhuash is that good weather periods tend to last for quite a while. Weather windows of five to six days are common.” During their climb, for example, they enjoyed acceptable weather, with some rain and snow but no high winds.

They warn that conditions in the Andes are changing quickly. “You need to observe the mountains and adapt,” Toralles said. “Some people aim for a route based on the information in a guidebook printed 50 years ago, and find a totally different scenario.” He pointed out that Siula Grande looked different in 2025 than it did when he climbed it just three years earlier.

Sanmartin noted that conditions have also changed on normal routes. They are now drier, more complex, and often more hazardous. “In some cases, climbers are abandoning the normal routes up the glaciers and opt for ridges which may be more difficult but in better conditions,” he said.

The climbers think the Andes will not get as crowded as the Himalaya, simply because the logistics are not as advanced — no refuges, no helicopters — and because they don’t have the sizzle of being the tallest peaks on Earth. “But they have incredible potential,” they said.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8280. Causing a Scene: North Meets South on the AT

Even though the Franklin free hiker shuttle isn’t until late morning, all of us at the Rock Gap shelter wake up early. We only have 3.7 miles to hike, but are feeling the eagerness of Town Energy. Ahead of the pack, Show and I descend the final steps to Winding Stair Gap at little after 8:30AM. There’s nothing to do but wait, for the next couple hours. Unless…

“What the hell,” I say, walking away from the woods towards the highway shoulder. “It doesn’t hurt to try. What else are we gonna do for two hours?”

As an approaching vehicle appears, rounding the corner in the distance, I stick out my thumb and spread a calculated I-want-something-from-you smile on my face that will hopefully be perceived instead as genuine friendliness. The car speeds past, making no efforts to slow down. Its windy wake rustles my hair across my cheek.

Usually I hate hitch-hiking. The stakes of needing to get somewhere and not knowing if or when you actually will get there feel too high to my control-freak brain. But in this scenario, with the fail-proof backup plan of the town’s shuttle coming, eventually, for certain, I’m willing to test my luck.

I drop my pack on the ground in front of me, so I don’t have to keep standing there with it on, and so cars can more easily identify me as a thru-hiker. Show walks over to join in, standing behind me so I’ll be the first person in view. The age-old trick of using the female hiker as bait.

I’m not convinced this actually works. I’ve been in many a scenario where a single guy or two men have gotten rides quicker than Show and I have. And yes, I have tried various womanly wiles such as letting my hair down when it was long and standing there in a sports bra. Neither has definitively provided a quicker ride. We do it like this anyway, just in case we happen to get a driver who perceives picking up a woman as less threatening. Or believes me to be a “damsel in distress” and wants to come to my unnecessary rescue.

Ten minutes later we’re still standing there. Raising our thumbs and curling our lips every time a vehicle appears, dropping them immediately as it blows past us. I’m not discouraged, though. We’d expected to be waiting here anyway.

As a promising pickup truck rushes by, and I swear, may have even sped up at seeing us, Rufio, Cous Cous, and Noah pop out of the woods at the trailhead. They walk over to join in on the hitching, the five of us standing in a line with our thumbs out. Now we really need a pickup to fit everyone.

A few minutes later, a silver truck approaches from the opposite direction. We don’t bother to display our thumbs since it’s not heading the way we’re trying to go. To our surprise, it begins slowing down and pulls into the parking lot. Are they here…for us? That awkward moment of trying to decipher whether you should be presumptuous and start walking over towards the vehicle, or if the person is coincidentally arriving to go for a hike.

Fortunately this driver makes it obvious by pulling up to where we’re standing and rolling his window down. A gentleman in his 60s or 70s pokes his head out and says, “When I first drove by there were only two of you. Then I turn around and there’s five! If someone’s willing to ride in the bed I can take everyone.”

We quickly decide amongst ourselves that Cous Cous is going to take the bed. He’s the greenest of all of us and has never ridden in the back of a pickup truck before. Let’s get this kid some life experience! We load up the bed with all of our packs and Cous Cous climbs in next to them. Show, Noah, and I take the back seat. Rufio rides shotgun.

As we drive past vistas of evergreen mountain views towards Franklin, the driver shares with us that God wanted him to pick us up. He further explains that he’s always on the lookout for ways to provide random acts of kindness on His Holy’s behalf. Seeing us hitching was today’s “God shot.” Hey man, whatever got you to pull over, I’m here for it.

Observing the driver and Rufio sitting next to each other, a devout Christian and a hippie ex-con, strikes me as humorous. An unlikely duo to be en route together. But with Rufio addressing him politely as “sir” in his slight Texan drawl, it kind of works anyhow. It’s a beautiful thing that the trail brings so many folks together who ordinarily wouldn’t have anything to do with each other.

When the man drops us off in town he insists on a prayer circle before departing. We kind of owe him one so no one objects. We gather round behind the tailgate and bow our heads. He thanks God for providing an opportunity to help us and asks Him to bless our journey ahead. We close with an Amen, thank the man, and head towards the local diner for breakfast.

The small town vibe in the Kountry Kitchen was palpable. It’s the kind of joint where the elderly townies gather at 6AM open for a cup of Joe seven days a week. They banter with the waitresses and everyone knows each other by name. The sort of place where your local-ad-decorated mug is never fully empty because a kind woman always seems to appear with a carafe at your side by the time you’re running half full. “More coffee, hon?”

We get seated at a large table towards the back, segregating us and our smell from the other patrons. A reasonable choice by the wait staff. On the wall above the table is a poster that reads “All I need is a little bit of coffee and a whole lot of Jesus.” It makes me laugh to myself because I’ve seen it before in an entirely different context.

There’s these hilarious gay husbands who have made an Instagram Reel series out of going into Homegoods stores and reading out signs such as these to each other in an exaggerated, sassy manner. You can’t help but smile at the dramatics and the way they crack each other up. This exact sign made an appearance in one of the videos, so I can’t take it seriously. Here, down south, though? These people didn’t hang that up ironically. They’re really that big on Jesus.

Another thing they’re apparently big on down south? Eavesdropping. I can’t remember what exactly Rufio was doing or saying, but it doesn’t really matter. He was talking loudly (likely with some cuss words thrown in), and just generally being his boisterous self. Noah, a born and raised southern boy, became visibly uncomfortable with this occurring in the restaurant. “Hey,” he said to Rufio, “just keep it down in here, we don’t want to cause a scene. People down here love to eavesdrop and they’re definitely going to be judging us if we’re acting out.”

“Bruh, I AM the scene!” Rufio responded unapologetically, but he turned it down a notch, as best as he personally could.

“Yo!” Show exclaimed. “Now that should be your trail name. The Scene!” We all agreed it would be funny to have both “The Show” and “The Scene,” an inevitably dynamic duo, in the group. But alas, he’d already fully embraced the name Rufio and was unwilling to change it. We did, however, occasionally refer to him as “The Scene” going forward as a sort of secondary trail name since it was so fitting to his outspoken and flamboyant nature.

I found this interaction to be an interesting example of how different the social culture is in the southern versus northern US. Going off what I learned from Noah, it seems southerners have a tendency towards listening-in, gossiping, and generally being all up in each others’ business. At least in small towns such as this one. As a lifelong northerner, in my experience these behaviors are not non-existent, but much less common. Up north we tend to keep to ourselves more, mind our own business.

We also may have different definitions of and tolerances for “causing a scene.” Personally, if I were to go into a casual restaurant in New England and someone was being a little loud and/or swearing in public, I wouldn’t think much of it. Unless of course it was something that was continuous for a long period of time, that’s obnoxious anywhere. But I wouldn’t bat an eye at a brief raised voice or an expletive shouted out here and there. I also wouldn’t think to intentionally listen-in on a conversation happening at the next table, nor consider that others in my surroundings are purposefully trying to listen-in on mine. And even if they are, why the hell would I care? It’s just not a thing here.

If you’re from either the north or the south in the US, weigh in! Do you agree with these differences in social norms around public politeness and business-minding (or lack thereof)? Let me know your experiences in the comments!

I recognize that may be a jarring headline for all of my southern readers. “Forgive my northern attitude,” as Noah Kahan would say. Promise I’m not being a deliberately crass northerner for titling this section that. Believe it or not, Ballsack is actually a person I want to tell y’all about.

If you follow a lot of thru-hiking content, you likely know that in recent years there are have been a number of Calendar Year Triple Crowners out on these trails. In case that title itself isn’t self-explanatory, these are folks who attempt to hike all three Triple Crown trails in one calendar year. Some even do what’s called a “Border to Border” Calendar Year Triple Crown (B2BCYTC) by extending the AT in both directions to touch the Maine/Canadian border and the southernmost tip of the US in Key West, Florida.

In 2025, we had Peg Leg and Punisher. This year, among the B2BCYTC-ers out there was (well, still is, as far as I know, since it is November at the time of this writing) a woman by the trail name of Ballsack.

I’ve been following Ballsack on Instagram for a number of years now, ever since I discovered her account while she was posting about her first AT thru, which I believe was back circa 2021. A woman named Ballsack? Immediately I was amused and intrigued. I came for the name, but stayed for her delightfully silly personality and raw, honest content.

Not only was she posting photos from her various thru-hikes, but sharing a lot of writing about them. Anyone who posts well-written, long-winded captions on Instagram captures my attention, and she was no exception. I noticed one thing in particular about her content creation that she was doing differently than most others in the thru-hiking community. She was posting after as opposed to during.

Ballsack has been highly unapologetic and forthcoming about this choice of hers to do so. I regularly saw posts explaining her desire to live in the moment during the hike, and then separately process her experiences in writing, in hindsight, later on. If this idea sounds familiar to my style of trail content creation, well, this is the source.

On the CDT, when I was struggling to come to terms with the fact that blogging and hiking simultaneously wasn’t working for me, I drew on Ballsack’s example. I knew instinctively that this way was the answer, but was afraid if I posted after the fact that my audience would care less about my content because it wasn’t being presented in real time. As if time would make it obsolete. Why would anyone want to read about last year’s hike when other people are on to posting about this year’s?

Witnessing someone else doing it and maintaining a decently large following on socials made me realize that it can be done. People still care. They’re willing to wait for quality content if they like it enough, regardless of a timing gap between the happening of the thing and the recording of it. Following Ballsack’s example, I was finally able to grant myself permission to do things my way instead of how I “should” be doing it. I have no regrets. The blog is better for it, the hikes have been better for it, and I’m better for it.

Huge shout out to Ballsack—if you happen to read this, although I’m sure you won’t, because you’re a little busy right now hiking 10,000 miles!!—you’ve been a huge inspiration to me and my trail writing! Thank you for normalizing hindsight content so I could feel confident doing it too.

Blogging my CDT hike from home. I may not be able to walk and blog at the same time figuratively, but hindsight content allows me to do it LITERALLY.

While Ballsack and I follow each other and had interacted once or twice on Instagram, we’d never crossed paths in real life. Until, with no prior warning, the door to Kountry Kitchen opened and she walked in.

I’d of course known she was a couple months into her B2BCYTC by now. I figured there was a good chance she’d pass us at some point on the AT this year. But in my own busyness with pre-trail preparations I’d fallen off with keeping up with any updates she was posting online. The last I’d known she was still on the Florida Trail. So even if she’d finished that, she still had everything between its northern terminus and Springer to complete. It could be a bit until she caught up to us. Wow, she’s already here? She’s cruising along! I remember thinking. (As it turned out, she had skipped ahead to start the AT and would return for the mileage gap later on.)

Having finished our breakfast, I got up to pay at the register. After checking out, I turned to walk back to our table and saw Ballsack standing next to it, chatting with our gang. As I approached, I exclaimed in excitement, “Omg it’s Ballsack!” saying the word far too loud for the southernness of the space. I’m sure any eavesdropping old ladies brunching after Bible study clutched their pearls and Noah internally cringed.

Ballsack turned to greet me. I could see searching recognition on her face. I knew she knew she knew me but couldn’t place me. “I’m Toddler Snacks,” I let her off the hook, “we follow each other on Instagram.” She nodded as it clicked and we expressed our IRL nice to meet yous.

“I’m Cous Cous,” Cous Cous said when it was his turn.

“But what’s your full name…?” I prompted, goading him playfully.

“Ughhhh,” he groaned in angst, as we all giggled. “It’s Cous Cous Sunset.” He rolled his eyes, his disapproval evident in the emphasis of “sunset.” He hadn’t fully accepted the full name yet at this point.

I don’t think Ballsack knew what to make of this with no context. After a few more minutes of chatting she said she had to get going to do some town chores, but would see us later at the hostel, where we were all staying that night.  The rest of us got up to do the same and we parted ways.

Our first errand in downtown Franklin was stopping by Outdoor 76, a famous outdoor outfitter that caters towards AT thru-hikers. It’s a place many novice NOBOs rely on to swap out gear that hasn’t worked out after its 110 mile field test, myself included. In 2020, I remember purchasing a new sleeping pad here. The one I’d started out with, and confidently elected not to take on any shakedowns beforehand, turned out to be far more uncomfortable than my theoretical imaginings of sleeping soundly on it for 2200 miles.

Shoes are another major gear item NOBOs buy here. Just over 100 miles in, hikers often assess their foot pain status and decide whether their pre-chosen footwear is worth giving more time or switching up. The Outdoor 76 staff seem to be particularly knowledgeable in this department. I remember witnessing my 2020 tramily member, Spreadsheet, getting the full run down from one of the employees.

The gist of his shoe spiel that doubled as a pep talk was that by nature of hiking over 2,000 miles, we classify as “elite athletes” (his words) and we ought to look at ourselves that way. He stressed both wearing the correct footwear, and perhaps even more importantly, keeping our mileage down in the beginning, to prioritize recovery and prevent overuse injuries. I recall him specifically suggesting that our daily mileage not exceed 10 miles yet, which seems like a rather low number to many thru-hikers, especially the younger generations.

Having the experience I do now, I agree with this. Not the ten mile limit in particular. I think that should be determined on an individual basis according to your fitness foundation going in, thru-hiking experience level, pack weight, age, and other personal factors, as necessary. But I agree with the general sentiment of starting SLOW; more so than you think you need to. You can always speed things up later on once you have trail legs. I’ve seen enough people burn themselves out in the first few hundred miles to know that slow is the way to go at the start of a thru.

This time around, in 2026, I was dialed in on my starting kit, so there wasn’t anything that I needed to replace this early on. As I browsed the store for shits and giggles, something caught my eye. Are those…dino nugs??

I looked longingly at the dino nugs pair thinking, ugh, I love these. They literally have toddler snacks on them… But I didn’t think I could justify carrying another pair of shorts in my pack. With sadness I left them behind. Show and I signed our names on the banner beneath the register and walked out of the store towards the hostel.

The hostel was different from what I remembered of it on my 2020 hike. It had changed ownership, names (slightly), and layout. Six years ago it was called “The Gooder Grove” and was run in the upstairs portion of the single-family house by an eccentric man by the name of Zen, who seemed nice enough but had gone down one too many rabbit holes of conspiracy theories. Now, the hostel resided in the basement of the house, was being hosted by a hospitable former thru-hiker couple named Willow and Bluebs, and had been renamed simply “The Grove.”

After dropping off our packs, gathering our laundry for the staff to wash, and taking showers, Show, Noah, and I headed back out for our resupply run at the Ingles. We wandered down the streets of Franklin in assorted loaner clothes and ended up getting a bit lost trying to figure out how to get around a widespread construction zone that the grocery store was on the other side of. Lucky for us, Bluebs spontaneously pulled up alongside us in his SUV. He informed us we were going a roundabout way and offered to drop us off at Ingles. We graciously accepted the ride.

At Ingles we ran into Sauce, Bill, and Cous Cous. Once done with our shopping, all of us besides Cous Cous (who was still inside) loitered outside the store next to the “No Loitering” sign, waiting for him so we could all walk back to the hostel together. The topic of Cous Cous’ broke nineteen-year-old status came up. We were all in agreement that, as older and more financially stable adults, we wanted to help him out here and there. We’d all heard him express his money woes and concerns over whether or not he could feasibly finish the trail on a modest $1,500 budget.

“I’m going to run back in and swipe my card for his resupply,” Sauce announced, and walked back towards the doors.

“Good one!” I said. “Let’s get this kid to Maine!”

A few moments later, the two of them emerged together, Sauce looking triumphant and Cous Cous irritable. “I don’t like when you guys do that!” he protested futilely. We paid no mind to his disapproval. I’d already bought his breakfast that morning, which he didn’t like either. It seemed he was quite uncomfortable accepting what he perceived as handouts. From what little I knew of his history, he’d been financially independent from a young age and worked hard for everything he had.

I want to mention that I hesitantly included this last anecdote. I neither want to sing my own praises, nor present Cous Cous as merely “the kid” or a charity case. Cous Cous was an integral member of the group. Speaking for myself, I enjoyed witnessing his genuine care for others, intellect and depth (especially as a rather young person), and his growing self-confidence as a backpacker. Sure, we all liked to joke around, bust his balls, and mildly embarrass him occasionally in an older sibling way.

But the truth of the matter is, and I believe I can speak for the whole group when I say this, we liked having him around and viewed him as an equal. And thus, all of us shared the sentiment that we wanted to share our resources with him. When it’s no setback to you personally, why wouldn’t you want to help someone else out that you care about? Hopefully he’d get used to the help. Because it was going to keep happening.

Later, back at the hostel, we repackaged our resupplies and organized our freshly laundered belongings for the next day. It was a relaxing evening of preparation and socialization. Show even got some doggo snuggles in with Willow and Bluebs’ good boy.

I can’t remember his name – if you know, comment it down below!

We hung out with the other hikers, including Ballsack who showed up later on. I enjoyed talking shop with her about thru-hiking and discovering, as two New Englanders, we both shared the same loving appreciation for the Appalachian Trail. Both of us feel a sense of protectiveness bordering on defensiveness towards it. It’s our attempt to dispel the narrative that the AT is nothing but a more miserable and/or less beautiful experience than other trails that Triple Crown hopefuls bemoan having to “get through” to get their title. This is wholly untrue in my opinion, and it’s nice to have an ally in this belief.

She asked me what I thought of the CDT. At this point that was the one Triple Crown trail she had yet to do any of, as far as I know. I remember responding that it was beautiful but “a grind.” Which was my truth, as I experienced it. However, Show pointed out to me later on that this may have been in poor taste to say to someone who was attempting to hike over 10,000 miles in one year. Now THAT’S a freaking grind. My apologies to Ballsack if this came across as tone-deaf or discouraging. I’m still thinking about it eight months later.

In the morning Show and I got on one of the first shuttles back to Winding Stair Gap. We rode with Ballsack and a few others, I have no recollection who. Ballsack mentioned she was attempting to hike all the way to the NOC (Nantahala Outdoor Center) that day. It would be a little over 27 mile day for her, a distance we planned to cover in two full days and a nero, for perspective.

We figured she’d be long gone as soon as she entered the woods ahead of us, and told her as much when we unexpectedly bumped into her one more time at a stream crossing. She explained that she likes to take it slow and steady, hiking for more hours of the day rather than a fast pace to get all the miles in. Definitely a strategy I could get behind. It’s probably smarter not to push yourself too hard when you’ve got to do this nearly every day for an entire year.

Bear scat seen just north of Winding Stair Gap. No source teddies were seen.

Around lunchtime Show and I reached Wayah Bald, a popular day hike vista with a stone observation tower you can ascend to take in the 360 degree views of Nantahala National Forest. On my 2020 thru-hike this viewpoint was socked-in; I recall hiding out in the tower to escape the wind and mist while eating lunch. Today, there was some cloud cover and general moodiness in the skies, but the layered mountain views were on full display. I was grateful to again (as on Blood Mountain) get to see views I missed last time. Two for two so far on the view re-dos!

On account of it being early afternoon and our bodies feeling good, Show and I decided to push past our initial plan of staying at the Wayah Shelter. I texted the Dump Truckers group chat letting Leigh, Noah, and Cous Cous know our change of plans since we’d agreed to meet up there that night. While we were at The Grove in Franklin, Leigh had stayed somewhere else with family. We’d left Noah behind a few hours ago as he was waiting at a road crossing for a friend who was coming out to hike with him for a night. Cous Cous I believe had gotten on a later shuttle that morning and hadn’t yet caught up to us.

As we continued hiking to our new home for the night, the Cold Spring Shelter, we suddenly began to see an increasing amount of burned forest floor alongside the trail. Soon, we were in a charred wasteland, blackened leaf duff and singed rhododendrons all around us. Only the tree stumps and the smooth dirt trail had remained unaffected. What the heck?

Burn zones aren’t an uncommon thing to see on a thru-hike out west. I’ve walked through many before on both the PCT and CDT. They are, however, much rarer to see on the AT, and east coast in general. Due to the wetter, more humid climate, there are simply less forest fires over here.

But this didn’t look like the aftermath of a rampant wildfire based on my experience. Typically wildfires leave behind massive swaths of inches-deep dusty ash mixed with chunks of charcoal, as well as blackened dead trees, some still standing and many as downed logs. Here the forest floor was too neatly charred at surface level, as though someone had walked through with a blow torch. And the trees had been too conveniently spared, all upright, appearing alive and well. A controlled burn perhaps?

Downed logs in an old wildfire burn scar in the Bob Marshall Wilderness in Montana on the CDT in 2025.

Charred trees in a Northern California burn scar on the PCT in 2023.

Not long later we reached a sign that confirmed my suspicions, warning hikers that the area beyond was closed for a prescribed burn on March 6th. I checked my watch, knowing we were right around that date. Shit, hopefully it’s not the 6th, I thought.

“Today is…the 7th. Ok good. So they did this yesterday. Wow, good thing our schedule just so happened to put us here today. Had no idea this was even happening,” I said to Show.

“Yeah,” he agreed, “that would have sucked if we got all the way out here and had to turn around…”

We pressed on past the sign further into the burn zone and began to smell campfire smoke. From a ledge where there was a break in the trees we could see a large plume of ashen gray smoke coming from the woods at the bottom of the cliff. “Guess they’re working on that area down there today,” I observed.

Further down the trail we came upon a jumble of blackened logs that was emitting small wisps of white smoke. There were no flames, but everything else we’d seen up to this point had been fully extinguished. This was, albeit minimally, still actively burning.

Uhhh, is this supposed to be smoking still?” I halted and looked back at Show.

“Well, seeing as we know it’s a prescribed burn… I’m sure it’s fine to just… leave it alone?” I reasoned.

Had we found this smoking log just in and of itself, outside of that context, I probably would have felt more compelled to intervene in some way. I was further comforted in this choice not to a half mile or so later when we passed a couple members of a wildland fire crew. Cool, I thought, so they are still out here monitoring this situation. That’s good to know.

After a solid 15.8 miles of hiking, five of those extra and unplanned, Show and I were the first to arrive at the Cold Spring Shelter. It was a smaller than average three-walled log cabin lean-to with a metal roof. We’d seen a chance of overnight rain in the forecast so we were thankful there was still shelter space. By the looks of it, I guestimated four hikers could fit comfortably, five snugly, six sardines if you were really desperate.

We ended up with five in the shelter that night: myself, Show, Cous Cous, Noah, and his friend Matt. Leigh had texted us back that she was only going to make it to the Wayah shelter that night so luckily we didn’t have to squeeze one more. We actually ended up apologetically turning away a few people who showed up later. With all of our pads set up it turned out that even five was pushing it.

Everyone bedded down for the night, I read a book on the Kindle app on my phone, as I always do to help me fall asleep. My eyes grew heavier and it became increasingly hard to focus on the page and comprehend what was going on in the story—my cue reading time was over. I set my phone down on the shelter floor by my pillow and drifted to sleep.

Rustling sounds. I startle awake. Something crinkling above. My eyes dart to my pack, hanging up under the roof awning, suspended a few feet above my head. I strain to hear against the light rain plopping down on the metal roof and to see through the blackness. The rustling starts again. Yup, its coming from my pack, I confirm. Grabbing my headlamp, I shine its red light towards my pack. The bottom of my pack cover is twitching slightly. Fuck, I think, mice.

I look over at Show. He’s wide awake, as I expected. “I think there’s a mouse in my pack cover,” I whisper.

“Yeah, I can hear it,” he whispers back, “One ran across my pillow earlier. I haven’t slept at all.”

As quietly as I can manage, I slither out of the confines of my sleeping bag and shake my dangling pack. A small fluff ball drops to the shelter floor and scurries out of sight. I remove my pack cover and stuff it in the outer pocket of my pack. At least now if the mouse gets back on my pack it won’t sound like a potato chip bag crinkling anymore.

I get back in my sleeping bag and try to settle down, attempting to push down the anxiety around Show having a sleepless night. He absolutely hates mice. I knew right then this might be our last shelter sleep. Prior to the trail he had wearily conceded to give the shelters a chance on my persuasion. I had compromised that if we encountered any mice issues we would from there forward strictly stick to the tent (his preference). Welp, it was a good almost two week run. Better mentally prepare myself for packing up a cold, wet tent from now on. Possibly sometimes with rain actively falling on me while doing so. Lovely…

I’m just fading into new sleep when I jolt awake, again. This time it isn’t a mouse, but some commotion occurring outside the shelter’s opening. Someone is setting up a tent directly in front of the shelter. It’s still lightly raining; I can see the drops illuminated in their headlamp. I look at my watch: 10:30PM.

I roll over to face Show. He’s on his stomach, head propped up on his hands, looking out the shelter at the tent-pitcher.

“What is he doing?” I ask, flopping onto my stomach for a better vantage point. The tent is partially erected, and Noah is continuously running out if view into the darkness of the woods, then returning and securing a stake down, repeat. Ohh-kayyy…?

“Dunno,” Show responds. “I guess he didn’t want to be in the shelter anymore. I’ve been watching this whole setup process. It’s been pretty entertaining since I still can’t sleep anyway.”

“Sorry,” I sympathize. “We don’t have to sleep in the shelters anymore.”

“Yeah,” Show sighs. “I think I’m done with them.”

Tent finally pitched, Noah dips back into the shelter. “All good man?” Show asks him.

“Yeah, it’s just too squeezy in here. I couldn’t sleep so I decided to just set up my tent. But I forgot my stakes at the hostel so I had to find sticks.”

Ah, that explains the trips back and forth from the woods. We all bid each other another goodnight as Noah grabs his sleeping setup and trots out to his tent with it. By this point my mind is fully awake and alert. I figure the only way to shut it back down is to do the sleep routine all over again. I feel around for my phone, find it, and open the Kindle app.

After another hour or so of reading, my eyes once again grow heavy and out of focus. Show is snoring lightly next to me. All is well with the shelter dwellers. I fall asleep.

Hey, I’m Kelly aka Toddler Snacks! I'm a Triple Crowner with over 9,900 miles of thru-hiking experience under my hip belt on the AT (NOBO 2020 and 2026), LT (NOBO 2021), PCT (SNOBO/Flip Flop 2023), and CDT (SOBO 2025). I am a 35-year-old New Englander, currently living in NH. I'm proudly 4 years in recovery from alcohol addiction, neurodivergent (ADHD), and child free by choice (though I hope to someday be a fur mom). When I’m not hiking, I enjoy thrifting, reselling vintage & secondhand clothing online, crocheting, writing, and home reno projects. #8278. The Pentagon Wants Public Lands ‘Offered Up’ for a New Army Base. Wyoming Is Interested

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 19, 2026 6:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth wants to build a new military base, and some public land states like Wyoming say they have just the spots.

The base would house between 15,000 and 25,000 active-duty service members, according to a red, white and blue competition flier for what Hegseth is calling the Next Great American Base contest. And ideally it would include 100,000 to 250,000 contiguous acres.

Hegseth said the base should be a “dynamic, self-sustaining power projection platform” with its own power generation, modern air defenses, and advanced technology, reported Newsweek in early October, when the call for pitches first arrived. He went on to say the best proposals would cut through “the red tape, offering up the land and rolling out the red carpet for our military families.”

But much of the “red tape” associated with building a base on hundreds of thousands of acres of unoccupied land is likely federal government red tape. And no clear path currently exists for how to requisition and build a new military installation on federal public land, said Mark Squillace, a natural resources law professor at the University of Colorado Law School.

The Department of Defense would need to work around current Bureau of Land Management or U.S. Forest Service management plans that include stipulations for wildlife like migration corridors and winter range closures.

It’s also unclear if the base needs to support the U.S. Navy, which would limit public-land options since the bulk of the country’s public lands are in the Interior West like Nevada, Idaho and Utah, though the fact sheet says all locations, even those without water access, are on the table.

The DOD already administers 8.4 million acres in the U.S., but is looking to acquire lands managed by other agencies.

Wyoming, for its part, is eyeing both state and federal land for the base. The state’s adjutant general, Maj. Gen. Greg Porter, told a legislative committee in early November that Wyoming has identified at least four “chunks” of state and federal land, as reported by the Cowboy State Daily last week. Which chunks have not yet been disclosed.

Squillace lined out how building a military base on public land could — or could not — become a reality.

The DOD would first likely need to discuss any public land acquisition with the BLM or Forest Service. If the land use agency agrees that a military base would fit the current land management plan, it would need to prepare a draft Environmental Impact Statement that brings up potential complications and confirms compliance with laws like the Endangered Species Act or National Historic Preservation Act.

“A positive review would likely lead to temporary withdrawal of the land from the operation of certain public land laws (like mining and leasing) that could interfere with the operation of the base,” Squillace says, while also noting that existing rights would be protected. Any particularly valuable mining claims would have to be bought out in what he describes as a “time consuming and expensive” process.

Read Next: Senate Poised to Vote on Affordability Package that Hunters, Anglers Worry Is a Trojan Horse for Public-Land Rollbacks

Ultimately, however, Squillace says any large-scale, long-term use of land would likely need to be approved by Congress, “since such transfers of public land are not authorized under law.”  Whether or not the dramatically different Congress to be sworn in next year would green light a public-land transfer requested by Hegseth also remains uncertain.

Christine Peterson is a freelance writer based in southeast Wyoming covering hunting, fishing, outdoor recreation, wildlife and the environment for Outdoor Life, High Country News, National Geographic and others.

#8275. Lucky Skier Survives Night in Crevasse With Two Broken Legs

Thirty-nine-year-old Emeric Ossatte was ski touring alone earlier this week in the Savoie area of the French Alps, near the Italian border. He removed his skis to posthole up a steep couloir in his boots and crampons when an avalanche swept him downhill, depositing him into a crevasse and breaking both his legs.

The Grenoble-area man, who wasn’t carrying a beacon or radio, doffed his backpack and crawled 100m through the crevasse, looking for a way out. But the sides of the crack were too steep, and there was no cell service where he was. He remained beside an air pocket in the crevasse overnight. Meanwhile, when he didn’t check in that evening, his family raised the alarm.

According to a Facebook report by France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue unit, they saw the slide path but no immediate sign of Ossatte. The search continued until the middle of the night, when avalanche risk forced the rescuers to suspend their operations.

The following morning, a large contingent of 28 ground rescuers, dogs, three helicopters, and a physician to tend to Ossatte, if they found him alive, converged on the site, according to France 3. They found his gear and dug for five hours until they heard him calling out.

“He was really lucky,” said Major Yoann Haberey of the mountain-rescue team. “We knew he was under there. But we didn’t think we’d find him alive.”

By the time they extracted him, he had been there almost a day and a half. The hypothermic but lucky survivor was flown to the hospital for treatment and surgery on one of his legs.

#8274. ‘Complete Shock.’ Long-Haul Trucker Hunter Tags 227-Inch Nontypical During State Park Hunt

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Updated Nov 19, 2026 6:37 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Indiana’s state park system offers a limited draw hunt that allows residents to target the abundant whitetails on park land that doesn’t get much hunting pressure. There are about 25 parks in the popular program, with two firearms hunts allowed on most of them. Each hunt runs just two days.

“I put in for the early two-day hunt on November 16 and 17 at a Northeast Indiana state park and got drawn for it,” Mike Miltroka tells Outdoor Life. “Trail cameras are not allowed on park [land]. But you can scout before hunt days. I scouted the park on Sunday, the day before the first day of hunting on November 16.”

Miltroka found a likely spot and, well before daybreak on Monday, he was in a tree saddle with his Ruger American Generation II chambered in 7mm PRC.

“It was cool and a little windy, but I was seeing deer soon after daybreak,” says Miltroka, a 38-year-old long-haul truck driver from Farmland. “I spotted a good buck far out, but the cover was too thick to shoot.”

As the morning wore on, Miltroka got a chance to take two does, and he dropped them both with his Ruger. He toted both does to his truck using a deer cart, since motorized vehicles are not allowed off-road.

The spot was already paying off, and Miltroka had a friend — who had seen a giant deer using the area before the hunt — to thank for tipping him off about it.

“I’d never seen the deer previously, but a buddy showed me a video [he’d taken] of the buck a month earlier while visiting the park … It was a place no one else was hunting, and I checked to make sure it was okay to hunt there – and it was.”

On the second day of the hunt, Miltroka chose a different tree. He climbed into a big walnut closer to the cover where he’d seen the buck on Monday. But unlike the day before, Miltroka didn’t see much except a coyote. Then, around 8:30, things changed.

“I heard something raking trees behind me, and I didn’t know what it was. I kept trying to see what was making all that racket, then I saw him out about 70 yards,” he says. “I knew it was a good shooter buck, and when I got him in my scope I squeezed the trigger, fired, and the deer took off running.”

Miltroka jacked another round into the chamber, but as soon as he picked up the running buck in his scope again, the deer went down.

It wasn’t until Miltroka studied the dead buck through his scope that he realized how immense the deer was.

“I started shaking, and yelling,” he says. “I got buck fever after it was over … I called a friend of mine and was so excited he asked if I was tied tight to my tree, and I said yes. I was shaking so much I might have fallen out of it.”

Once he was steady enough, Miltroka climbed down and walked up on the deer.

“I was in complete shock,” he says. “I’ve only taken about six bucks, and none even have the size of this one. I was screaming with joy.”

He filled out a transport tag for his buck, which filled his limit of three deer for the park hunt – only one of which can be a buck. Then he wheeled the buck to his vehicle, loaded it, and drove to park headquarters to check it in.

The buck’s field dressed weight was 181 pounds. The 22-point nontypical was green scored by an official Boone & Crockett measurer the day after Miltroka shot it. It gross scored 227 inches, with a net score of 197 6/8s inches. It has an outside antler spread of nearly 25 inches, and one main beam of the rack measures over 27 inches.

He will have the buck scored again after the required 60-day drying period for B&C and will enter the buck into B&C’s nontypical record category. He’ll also submit his buck to the Hoosier Record Buck Program.

“The park hunts are well run and there are plenty of deer,” says Miltroka. “I saw lots of deer being checked in during the hunts and some nice bucks.

Read Next: ‘Over the Moon.’ Ohio Hunter Shoots 215-Inch Buck from Her Favorite Stand on Her Birthday

“I’m stunned at the size of my deer. But there are so many great bucks that have come from Indiana.”

A correction was made on Nov. 19, 2025: A previous version of this article included photos from a different 200-plus inch buck the author is reporting on. It has been updated with the correct deer and deer hunter.

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8271. Weekend Warm-Up: Behind the Climb

As the title suggests, Behind the Climb brings viewers behind the scenes with Colin Haley and Tyler Karow as they take on the Southeast Ridge of Cerro Torre. Colin Haley has tried the route, which he calls a modern classic, three times already without success. He hopes that by teaming up with Tyler Karow, the fourth time will be the charm.

As the short film opens, Haley introduces us to the route in voiceover, while on-screen, he and Karow make the approach, crossing ice fields and fording streams. The route is divided into three main sections: the Haston Crack, Ice Towers, and Headwall. Haley was turned back twice by bad weather on the Ice Towers; neither of them has finished the Headwall.

The Headwall section of Cerro Torre. Photo: Screenshot

Hoping to catch the Ice Towers still frozen and the Headwall still warmed by the sun, they wake up so early that the sun hasn’t actually finished setting yet. At that point, it seems more accurate to say that they took an evening nap and then climbed through the night.

Interspersed with clips of climbing, Haley and Karow review the footage, sharing the little logistical details and impressions of the climb. The Ice Towers, which Karow recalls attempting many years ago, have changed since he last encountered them. Once, as the name implies, thick with ice, much of it has now melted. “In another ten years,” Karow predicts, they will “look drastically different.”

Ice or no, they complete the second section. This time, the weather cooperates. Haley takes over for the final section, the dastardly Headwall which has repeatedly eluded him. The climbing itself is going well, but melting snow above is making the rock run with water. This, he admits, is a significant disadvantage to their carefully timed plan.

Despite the wet holds, Haley makes good progress. With only a few pitches to go, the self-described pessimist Haley admits that he finally thinks they’ll make it to the top. When he does finally reach the summit, grinning widely at the camera, voiceover reveals he has already started worrying about the descent.

Keeping with the promise to go behind the scenes, the video doesn’t stop at the summit, where they stand victoriously and admire the views. We follow the pair as they rappel down.

At the bottom, when asked how they feel about the adventure, both men reply simply that their hands hurt. Not all of us can be poets.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8270. I Went Duck Hunting During the Infamous Armistice Day Storm, and Lived to Tell About It

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 9:00 AM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

This story, “Cold as Death,” appeared in the October 1955 issue of Outdoor Life.

There has never been anything along the upper Mississippi, before or since, like the Armistice Day storm of 1940.

It started out as a mild, cloudy morning with the temperature between 50 and 60. Fellows went duck hunting in shirt sleeves. The wind picked up velocity as the day went along, but not enough to get excited about — not until late afternoon. Then, with little warning, a full-scale prairie blizzard screamed in out of the northwest.

By dark the air was smoking with gale-driven snow, and the thermometer plunged near zero. Bitter winds blew all night. The weather experts said afterward that gusts hit 80 miles an hour. The storm laid a track of death and destruction across six states; it pounded ships to pieces on the Great Lakes, blocked roads, isolated towns, paralyzed cities, and buried cars. Hundreds were marooned on highways. A crack streamliner was 27 hours late into Minneapolis. And when the morning of November 12 broke, 20 duck hunters lay dead, frozen in stiff, grotesque poses — scattered on mud bars, sand spits, and willow-grown islands of the Mississippi bottoms between Red Wing, Minnesota, and La Crosse, Wisconsin.

I was among the luckier ones. I spent that terrible night with two companions under an overturned skiff on a mud bar — wet from head to foot, without fire or heavy clothing, pants frozen to my legs, and kept alive only by the body warmth of the men who lay huddled on both sides of me. The storm cost me six weeks of agony in a hospital and half of my left foot. But at least I survived.

I was 14 at the time, attending high school in my home town of Winona, Minnesota. We got out of classes at 11 o’clock because it was Armistice Day. I scurried for home, planning an afternoon of duck hunting.

The wind was already fairly fresh and mother was uneasy. “The storm warnings are up,” she told me while getting my lunch. But I grew up in Winona and knew the river, and at that age, bad weather didn’t worry me. The only thing it meant to me was good shooting. Ducks had been scarce all fall and maybe a blow would bring ’em down. So I hurried through lunch, impatient to get in a blind.

While I was loading my gear in the car, my cousin, Bob Stephens, showed up with a partner, Cal Wieczorek, and they decided to join me. Bob was 20, Cal about 19.

We drove to the Prairie Island spillway at the lower end of the Winona pool, where Dad kept his boat. It was a 16-footer, pointed at both ends, with a homemade bracket for an outboard. We decided to hunt a place known as the Firing Line, along the border of the Upper Mississippi Wildlife Refuge, three or four miles upstream. Hunters lined up there to intercept ducks flying in and out of the refuge. In stormy weather the pass-shooting was exceptionally good, and this looked like that kind of day.

The wind was blowing half a gale now and was beginning to bite. Ominous dark clouds raced overhead, and scuds of cold rain pelted down as we clamped our old three-horse outboard on the boat, cranked it up, and headed out into the choppy pool. The three of us were a heavy load, and the skiff made slow headway against the wind.

We didn’t give it a thought then, but we couldn’t have been more poorly prepared. I was wearing cotton clothes — pants, shirt, socks, and a jacket lighter than a hunting coat. I had overshoes over my leather shoes. Bob and Cal were dressed similarly, except for hip boots. Cal and I had gloves. Bob had a heavy raincoat, and I still believe that made the difference between living and dying for me.

We had one book of matches and no pocketknife. I was carrying a 16 gauge single-barrel Winchester; the other two had pumps. Because we intended to pass-shoot, we took no decoys.

Backed up by a series of low dams, the Mississippi is two to three miles wide in that section, flooding its bottoms and forming open lakes, meandering channels, small islands, bars, and rush beds. We threaded our way through this maze, wallowing in the heavy seas. It took us 1½ hours to cover the distance to the Firing Line.

The sky was full of ducks; they were barreling across the bottoms in crazy skeins, riding the wind, and turning and twisting below treetop height. It was plain they didn’t like the weather. Before the afternoon was over I actually swung at a greenhead with my gun as he hovered above my head, almost stationary, while bucking the gale.

When we finally got to the Firing Line it was too crowded. The blinds were only about 25 yards apart and all occupied. Ducks were rocketing back and forth over the marsh and there was a steady barrage of shooting. We decided to find another spot.

By the time we found it — on a willow-covered island with many rushes in front — we were beginning to lose interest. The wind was howling like a banshee, down off the high bluffs to the west, with sleet and fine snow in its teeth. We hunkered in the willows for almost an hour, killed four ducks, and decided we’d had enough. The cold was already bone-chilling, our hands were so stiff we could hardly handle our guns, and we were starting to worry about getting back to the landing.

Two strangers came by in a little duck boat; they told us they were scared. That settled it for us, but going home was easier said than done. None of us knew exactly where we were, and when we tried to shove away from shore, the seas threatened to swamp us. We finally dragged the skiff to the lee side and managed to get it afloat and start the motor. But before going 200 yards we ran aground on a mud bar, which we couldn’t skirt because of the wind and seas. We dragged the boat across the bar and started on again.

We did that about four times before finding ourselves by a big open slough and admitting we were lost. It was almost dark now and snowing so hard we couldn’t see 100 feet. We couldn’t even look into the wind — the storm cut our eyeballs like a sandblast. And the slough was a welter of dirty foaming water in which our skiff probably wouldn’t last more than a minute.

While we debated what to do, another boat loomed out of the stormy dusk, riding low with four hunters. Two of them were only a little older than I — one from my home room at school. The others were men who had hunted the river bottoms all their lives. When they said they knew the way to Prairie Island and could make it without swamping, we decided to follow.

But I couldn’t get the outboard started. Bob and Cal rowed, handicapped by cold-numbed hands, while I tinkered with it. We managed to keep the other boat in sight for a few minutes. Then, pitching and rolling, it disappeared and we didn’t see it again. When our frantic yells brought no answer we realized we were again on our own. It was days before we learned what happened to the other party. Their skiff had capsized when we lost sight of it. Three of the hunters died there, standing in the icy water, not knowing which way to go to dry land. By some miracle one of the boys survived the night and rescue parties found him on a near-by island at daybreak.

Only a few minutes after we lost that boat we were washed ashore on a sand spit. Bob and Cal leaped clear as we grounded and escaped a wetting. I was in the stern of the skiff, working on the outboard — right in the path of a wicked sea that smashed over us the instant we touched bottom.

I can’t describe the shock that icy dunking gave me. I clawed my way out of the swamped boat, and the wind cut like a braided whip. My legs wouldn’t hold me up, so I crawled across the mud until I was beyond reach of the waves and lay there, cold and weak.

Cal and Bob dragged the boat up on dry land, turned it over, and propped one side up to make a shelter. When they finished I crawled under it. My pants and jacket crackled as I moved. They had frozen stiff in the few minutes I lay exposed to the wind.

We still had only a vague idea of our location, but we knew that large open lake, which we couldn’t cross in our puny skiff, lay between us and any landing. We didn’t talk much about it; we didn’t need to. Live or die, we’d have to spend the night where we were.

Bob and Cal did everything possible. They gathered rushes and put them under the boat to keep me off the wet mud. Then they searched desperately for firewood, but there wasn’t so much as a scrub willow. They finally found a few pieces of water-logged driftwood, heaped them in a pyramid, and turned the outboard motor up to drain gas on it — a risky thing to do, except in a desperate emergency like ours. But the screaming wind whipped the gas into nowhere as fast as it ran out of the tank. They struck one match after another without getting a spark of fire.

The last match flared and died in the darkness, and they crawled under the boat and lay down on the wet rushes, one on each side of me. They spread their hunting coats over us and laid Bob’s raincoat on top. The raincoat kept the worst of the wind out and probably saved me from freezing.

We had little sense of time and hardly talked. Bob told me afterward that I spoke only two or three times during the night, and then I worried about the anxiety my parents must be feeling.

I shivered for what seemed like hours, with my hands wedged between my thighs, since that seemed to be the warmest place on my body. I prayed silently. I didn’t think much about dying. I believed we’d get through the night somehow and in the morning find our way home. I realized finally that all feeling was gone in my feet; they were wooden stumps, no longer a part of me. I must have dozed, for I recall waking up and feeling warm, but that lasted only a minute or two and then I was shivering again.

The thing I remember best was the unending scream of the wind. It never stopped raging — searching under the overturned boat, wailing, screeching, terribly alive, terribly cold.

Sometime in the night Bob remembered that he had two apples and two sandwiches in his hunting coat. They were frozen hard, but we gnawed at them and they seemed to help.

The sound of distant gunshots reached us several times, and once we thought we heard faint, far-off yells for help. But our own guns were so crusted with ice that we couldn’t fire them, and it was useless to shout.

We knew we weren’t the only hunters lost on the river bottom. Others were enduring the same torture. And we felt sure there must be rescue parties trying to find us, but we didn’t think we could count on them while the storm continued.

It was days before we learned all that happened that hard night. Rescue efforts began at dark, and men fought the storm and seas all night in skiffs and powerboats. Some even waded the sloughs to bring lost men to shore. It wasn’t a night for small craft, and no one will ever know how many boats were overturned, swamped, and driven back. But the search went on, hour after hour, heedless of the wind, cold, and snow.

The night was an awful ordeal for the hunters. One party huddled on a marshy island beside a fire started with dead grass and fed with more than 40 cedar decoys. Gas from an outboard was used to rekindle the blaze each time it died down. Another group marched, jumped, and sparred around a fire built from their boats. Still another found a patch of timber, got a fire going, and then shot dead branches off trees for fuel.

They were the lucky ones; others didn’t fare so well. Two died under an overturned boat, unable to see a bonfire 400 yards away. Two more reached shore along a railroad embankment, crawled halfway up the bank, fell and froze to death, too weak to walk to safety. Others gave up, lay down where they were, without fire or shelter, and died quickly. A few drowned. Most froze. When the last of the living were rescued, the grim search for bodies continued. It was three days before all the missing were accounted for and the final toll of 20 dead was tallied.

Toward morning the air cleared enough to let me glimpse lights on the Winona dam, a few miles away. They glimmered through the driving snow, tantalizing and mocking us, but at least we knew now which direction to go when daylight came. About that same time Bob remarked that his hands were frozen and he’d probably lose them. He said it quietly, as if it didn’t really matter.

At first light the wind seemed to lose its gale force. It was still blowing hard, but when we studied the lake in the gray light of morning, we decided our skiff might weather the seas.

We were in bad shape. Cal and Bob could barely stand up; I needed help to get on my feet. Our hands were stiff and useless. The spit where we spent the night was rimmed with a 50-foot belt of ice that made launching our boat a tough job. But we didn’t have much choice. We couldn’t live through the day on that bleak mud bar, and we had no way of knowing when help would come.

I crawled into the boat. Bob and Cal inched it off the bar, broke a path in the ice to open water, and rowed slowly into the lake. Their hands, however, were too numb for the oars and they lost control when the full force of wind and waves hit us.

Spray froze to the skiff, coating it with ice. My clothes were as stiff as armor, my legs lost all feeling, and I no longer minded the wind. In that condition, with the boat growing ever heavier from its load of ice, we were swept across the Winona pool to the Wisconsin side.

That should have ended things, but there was still more torment ahead. The marsh had frozen and we grounded on the new ice, 100 yards offshore. The ice gouged a big hole in the bow of the skiff, but it wouldn’t bear a man’s weight. Luckily the wind drove us up until the hole was above water, and there we sat with no way to get to shore.

It was there a search plane found us an hour later. Part of the feverish search that was under way for 50 miles up and down the river, that plane — a Cub trainer piloted by Max Conrad of Winona — had been in the air since shortly after daylight. It was shuttling across the bottoms, spotting lost hunters, empty boats, deserted gear and stranded dogs, and dropping sandwiches, whisky, matches, and cigarettes. It even dipped low enough to shout encouragement to the men as they were found. Nobody in the rescue teams did a better job.

Conrad flew low over us, wagging his wings, and hollered a message we couldn’t understand. Then he was gone, but before long we heard the steady throb of a powerboat and saw the Army Engineers’ launch, Chippewa.

She couldn’t reach us, for we were out of the river channel in shallow water. It took the crew almost an hour to break ice and get to us with a skiff. Before they were through, my dad and a friend of his, who had been looking for us all night, showed up in a powerful outboard. They carried me to the launch, peeled off my shoes and frozen clothing, and for all I was only 14, gave me a man-size slug of whisky. I took it like a man, too, and never knew I’d had it.

Bob spent a week in a Winona hospital, but his frozen hands came through all right. Cal went home that same afternoon. I had a long bout with gangrene and lost part of a foot. I also lost a lot of weight, dropping from 143 to 85 pounds during my recovery. Christmas was less than a week away when I left the hospital, and I guess that was the best Christmas I ever had.

I’m a teacher in La Crosse now and I still hunt ducks on the Mississippi bottoms whenever I get the chance. But, I wear special boots, even on the warmest days, and take along an emergency kit that contains wool socks, gloves, down-filled hat, compass, knife, matches in watertight container, and a small flare gun. I never go without it.

There are too many mistakes in this world to make the same one twice, and any time I’m tempted to be careless, I remember how the wind sounded that awful November night.

#8268. Wildfire Closes Section of the Appalachian Trail and Skyline Drive in Shenandoah National Park

An active wildfire in Shenandoah National Park has closed of a stretch of the Appalachian Trail and Skyline Drive on Tuesday, November 25, according to an announcement from the Appalachian Trail Conservancy (ATC).

The Appalachian Trail is closed until further notice between Beagle Gap (NOBO mile 870.6) and Sawmill Run Overlook (NOBO mile 875.6), including the Calf Mountain Shelter. Skyline Drive is also closed between the Loft Mountain gate and Rockfish Entrance Station.

Hikers traveling through open areas of Shenandoah should also prepare for parkwide fire restrictions that took effect on Saturday. Park officials implemented the rules due to exceptionally dry conditions and the potential for extreme fire behavior.

Shenandoah National Park North Entrance. Photo via Davey Nin

Under the current restrictions, open-air fires are prohibited, including fires in grates, grills, fire rings, and pits. Liquid or wood-fueled portable stoves are also not allowed, along with smoking on trails. Visitors who spot smoke or suspicious activity are asked to notify a park ranger or call the park’s communications center at 800-732-0911.

If you encounter fire or smoke while hiking, the ATC’s wildfire safety guide recommends evacuating immediately, leaving the area as quickly and safely as possible, even if it means backtracking or exiting on a side trail.

Once you reach a safe spot, call 911 to report the fire and share, to the best of your knowledge, the precise location using landmarks such as road crossings, shelters, summits, or rivers rather than relying only on AT mileage, which most dispatchers will not know. If you spot smoke on a nearby ridge and cannot tell whether it is a wildfire or a prescribed burn, still call 911 so dispatchers can contact the right agency.

To stay ahead of active closures, check the ATC’s Trail Updates page before heading out and sign up for text or email closure alerts through ATCamp, which does not require a registered hike.
