#8226. Camino Day 21: Hórreos and the Father of the Modern Camino

We leave so early from Casa Cruceiro de Ferreiros that we have to unlock the door and let ourselves out. I can tell Suzanne is itching to go, but we only have one headlamp, which effectively yokes us until dawn, or first coffee. Then she will rabbit away. The last few days we’ve not walked together, which is not to say we’re walking alone—there are 1,000s of pilgrims to walk with.

The Way isn’t that different from days past, and the *turigrinos* are getting harder to distinguish from the rest of us. Yes, they have their cute little daypacks, but the clothes aren’t shiny anymore, and I see a weariness, a gift of several days on trail.

We walk by stone fences on rolling, rural lanes, and I try to imagine what it must have been like when resources were so slim that split stone was the material available for fences. There is a gentle roll to The Way, and we pass the 100.000k marker to Santiago—unremarkably just there, with no fanfare, like 1,000s of others. The end is 62 miles away!

Time and millions of pilgrims have worn down the Camino

I am so fascinated by the hórreos of Galicia

We walk between six-foot high dry lay stone fences, and later on a sunken path, worn down by this pilgrimage. I admire hórreos of every make: stone, brick, wooden, and a mix. They seem randomly dropped more than thoughtfully placed, at odd angles with what is around them. I know that many have been here since the 13th century, but neither they nor the landscape yield to one another. Hórreos evolved to dry millet until the arrival of the New World crop: corn. Galicia’s climatic peculiarities prevent grain from completely drying in the field. Done mechanically today, in years past the ears were shucked and stored whole to allow air to pass through, to cool and dry the grain to prevent spoilage and fermentation.

In an information sheet I found at tonight’s hostel, it reads:

In 1973, a Decree was approved by the Spanish state to protect all the old granaries and huts existing in Galicia and Asturias, which attempted to address the consequences of the abandonment of the rural way of life and the loss of use of the granaries.

I guess I won’t be transporting any hórreos home to my backyard.

We’re in pulpo territory, and find pulperías and copper cauldrons smoking away outside roadside bars. The Galician preparation for octopus is smoked paprika, sea salt, and a splash of olive oil, all served on a seven-inch wooden plate and eaten with toothpicks. Suzanne largely doesn’t partake because the octopus is known to be an intelligent sea creature; I’d feel bad too if they weren’t so tasty. Kinda like bacon—if they are so smart, then why so tasty? It is a tragic, delicious bargain we’ve made with nature’s smartest species. On a lighter note, we’ve stumbled into a soft homemade cheese, queso con miel, that is drizzled with local Galician honey, and the seasonal padrón peppers.

Padrón peppers – fried with olive oil and sprinkled with course salt.

I walk with David and his mother Maxine, from Australia. She is a 12-month cancer survivor, and as David tells the story, when she was at her lowest… she interjects “so exhausted I couldn’t even hold a cell phone” David pitched walking the Camino. That was 18 months ago, and here they are. I think back to other Aussies I’ve met, and each seemed to be recovering from a major health crisis.

Then, there was Fritz, born in Nebraska, but sent away to boarding school at a young age and never returned, living abroad and traveling the world in the business of finance. He tells me a different side of the story of how the modern Camino came to be. His friend was named Manuel Fraga Iribarne, who in the mid-1990s was the regional governor of Galicia, then one of the poorest, most isolated agricultural regions of Spain. At that time, the Camino was a quiet trail mostly walked by devout Catholics, historians, or avid hikers. (When they invent time travel, this is where I’m returning, to walk the Camino of those days). Working with Father Elías (the priest of O Cebreiro), who was the spiritual father and painted the yellow arrows to guide the way, Fraga had the political and marketing know-how to rebuild the infrastructure we enjoy today. One hears of Father Elías in Catholic stories, but not Manuel Fraga Iribarne. Thank you, Fritz.

This is his eighth Camino, and he is reluctantly walking it because it is a graduation requirement for the two-week course he took in Santiago. Fritz has been so interesting to listen to and walk with, but I recognize in him that, like myself, much of our success has been given to us through the privilege of the stations our parents gave us. As men, much of how we see ourselves is through our work, and Fritz, David, and the other unhappy conservative I walked with are all now retired. Their importance and reason for being is gone, and I think they are looking for something that replaces what they have lost. SH*T, I think to myself, this is the third man I’ve walked with that turns out to be angry and older—what is she, The Camino, trying to teach me?

He has seen the world and chooses to be angry. I see his unwavering support of the current administration being driven more out of retribution for the direction the country was going than any hope or vision for its future. If our country was a garden and overrun by weeds, his solution would be to drown it in Roundup and pave it over. No more weeds. My Dad would say that weeds are just misplaced plants and can have a lot to say diagnostically about the health of the soil. The goal isn’t to keep a weed-free garden, but to produce food, knowing that for the health of the soil, weeds must coexist.

So I walk with Fritz until I can’t. Yes, he is a fast walker, unencumbered, and fresh on the Camino, but I tire of his raw anger, in such contrast to the Anglican faith he professes. My exit comes when he sees his pickup van in case he is ready to stop walking. He goes over to check in and I, who have no pickup van, sit and rest. Fritz continues on, walking over the hill and out of my Camino never to be seen by me again.

#8225. Climbers Forge New Route on Mount Shiva Despite Limited Acclimatization

Benjamin Bosshard and Philippo Sala will never forget their first experience above 6,000m and the new route they opened with Stefan Siegrist on India’s Bahai Jar, commonly known as Mount Shiva (6,142m).

Expeditions in the Indian Himalaya are usually quiet while they take place, because satellite phones and InReach devices are prohibited. After returning home, however, Stefan Siegrist reported the details, and the team’s cameraman, Christoph Detschmann, posted a video summary of the expedition:

Siegrist had already visited the Chenab River Valley area in 2018 and climbed Shiva with Jonas Schild, but he still had designs on its unclimbed northwest ridge. At the end of September, he, Sala, and Bosshard reached base camp in the Kishtwar massif.

The three Swiss climbers were prepared to endure a long period of bad weather while acclimatizing. To their surprise, a good weather window opened right away. It was too good to let go, despite not being properly acclimatized and Sala and Bosshard’s lack of altitude experience. The window was expected to last just three days. After many “discussions and doubts,” they left base camp on October 3.

Sala had a headache during the first night, but the following morning, they were all totally focused on the climb, and the tension disappeared. “We assumed that we were unlikely to reach the summit on this attempt, and that we would therefore be wasting our only chance before a major storm [brought] the season to an end,” Siegrist explained. “So from then on, the idea was simply to climb higher and acclimatize, without any pressure.”

A moment of the climb, filmed from a drone by Christoph Detschmann

Along the way, they discovered they had left their stove at base camp. No problem in 2026: The expedition photographer used his drone to carry the stove up to them. The use of drones was a hot topic during the recent Piolets d’Or: To what extent are they compatible with alpine-style criteria? While most accepted that drones could scout routes or sections out of view of base camp, many considered using them to carry equipment up a face to be a red line.

On Shiva, Siegrist says the delivery of the missing stove “proved essential to the success of the climb.”

The climbers spent their second night at 5,250m at the base of the northwest ridge. On the following morning, they had to decide whether to continue or retreat. They all felt well, but starting the highly technical ridge was a point of no return. At the same time, Siegrist recalls, they realized they really had a chance to summit. And so on they went.

That night, they stopped at 5,700m in what Siegrist describes as a crucial moment. His partners had not been at that altitude before, and the latest forecast announced a storm the following day. They decided to wait and see what the morning looked like. No one slept at all that night.

The last meters before the summit. Drone image by Christoph Detschmann

The sky was still clear when the team set off again at 4am, so they chose to keep going. “After a while, we finally reached the final slope, just 300m below the summit,” Siegrist wrote. By now, they knew they were going to make it. They reached the top at 7:44am, six days after arriving in Base Camp, and just in time to hurry down as the storm approached.

The climbers descended via the 1985 Japanese route (see topo below), and reached their base camp tents as it started to snow.

#8220. Two Inexperienced Teens Rescued After 20 Hours on Grossglockner

Two Germans, aged 15 and 18, spent almost 20 hours on Austria’s highest mountain before being evacuated by helicopter on November 15.

Initially, police reported that the pair had reached the 3,798m summit and become lost while descending. The younger climber disputes this, saying they turned back near an alpine hut at 3,454m because his companion felt ill.

Their ordeal began around 11pm on Saturday, November 14. The pair had no previous experience with such a serious mountain but had prepared by reading websites, watching videos, training physically, and asking others for tips. They did carry crampons, an ice axe, a helmet, a rope, a belay device, and extra clothing.

It took them a long time before they decided to turn around. The descent went well at first, but one spot seemed too dangerous to them. To get around it, they followed someone else’s tracks in the snow, but these suddenly disappeared. While looking for a way, they got stuck in deep snow. The older boy called for help at around 6:30pm on November 15, according to Austria’s public broadcaster, ORF.at.

At first, rescuers tried to guide the pair down by phone. More than two hours later, the boys were still stuck, so a helicopter was dispatched. The rescuers found the pair, exhausted, disoriented, and hypothermic, at around 2,800m.

A rescue helicopter brings the teens to safety. Photo: Mountain Rescue KALS

Yesterday, rescue leader Peter Tembler criticized their lack of alpine experience and preparation. He told the German Press Agency that the pair lacked sufficient experience for the terrain and winter conditions. He also criticized their late-night departure.

According to Tembler, a section that normally takes four to five hours took them almost 12. Other climbers reportedly advised them against continuing.

Tembler believes an increasing number of inexperienced people are attempting serious alpine routes, sometimes encouraged by social media accounts that downplay the difficulty. The disagreement has become a public debate about risk-taking, responsibility, and rescue operations.

As Austria’s highest peak, Grossglockner is a magnet for climbers of all stripes and has more than its share of controversy. In January 2025, a man abandoned his less experienced girlfriend high on the mountain. She froze to death, and an Austrian court later convicted him of manslaughter for leaving her behind. This summer, an altercation occurred on Grossglockner in which a guide insulted a passing climber and nearly pushed him off a cliff. At the same time, veteran alpinists are still setting bold new routes here.

According to Der Standard, Grossglockner gets 8,000 to 9,000 ascents each year, with an average of two dozen rescues annually. Between 2005 and 2025, 29 climbers have died on the mountain.

One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8219. Historic Drought Looms Over Arkansas Duck Opener. What Few Ducks Show Up Face Heavy Pressure

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 4:45 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

If you asked any serious waterfowl hunter to name their dream duck-hunting destinations, Arkansas would be at the top of nearly every list. Its classic flooded hardwoods and bayous along with the state’s rich rice and grain fields, lure massive flights of puddle ducks and geese to its hallowed waters every year. This has made Arkansas stand out above other southern states as the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway.

Yet in 2026, Arkansas’ waterfowl season is suddenly looking bleak. A historic drought has many duck hunters worried ahead of the Nov. 21 opener, as the serious lack of water could bring duck hunting to a standstill. Duck hunters on both public and private land may have some serious decisions to make to safeguard ducks and future duck seasons.

For 14 months Arkansas has been without any serious rainfall, leaving rich croplands and, more importantly for ducks, wetlands, drier than they’ve been in decades. Every corner of the state is under a severe drought status, with some areas being more than 20 inches behind in cumulative rainfall totals.

To put the drought in perspective, this is slated to be the state’s third-driest October through January since record keeping began in 1895.

This leaves the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission with very little water to spare, to the point where the agency expects it will only be able to flood around 40 of its more than 70 moist-soil public-land hunting units.

“In my 50 years of duck hunting in Arkansas, I don’t remember it ever being as dry as this,” says Brent Birch, founder of Arkansas’ Greenhead magazine. “It’s been this way all summer and well into the fall. And even with the prospects of a Super El Niño, we have nothing the forecast that says it’s going to resolve anytime soon.”

Without an incredible amount of rainfall in a short amount of time, Birch and other waterfowlers around the state are worried not only about not having places to hunt, but that the ducks may not even show up.

“So far, we are seeing a few groups of gadwalls, pintails, and other early migrating ducks but not in anything close to their usual numbers,” Birch says. “And when it comes to incoming mallards, you can pretty much forget it. It’s just too dry for them. The fact is the closer it gets to 2027 under these conditions, the worse the entire duck season is going to be. And if we don’t start getting some wet weather soon to push ducks down here, we’re looking at a poor outlook for the entire season.”

The reason that Arkansas waterfowl hunting is so good is that it lies at the north end of the historic feeding grounds for ducks and geese in the Mississippi Flyway. Once the cold sets in around their spring and summer breeding grounds in Canada, the birds come south to binge on Arkansas’ rich wetlands throughout the winter. However, with the current drought, the fear is that the incoming ducks will be forced to fly elsewhere.

“Ducks coming south to Arkansas aren’t like ducks in northern states,” says Ducks Unlimited Arkansas regional biologist Jake Spears, who lives in Jonesboro. “Northern birds are more apt to feed in dry fields so long as they have a body of water to roost on nearby. However, in the Southern U.S., they stop feeding in fields and primarily feed in water. They look for things like water plants and waste grains in flooded fields, which Arkansas currently just doesn’t have.”

Spears worries that many ducks are going to fly to Arkansas and then be forced to turn northward again or keep driving south.

“The ducks need food so they may go to Louisiana, or Kansas, or Missouri — wherever they can find that food source,” says Spears. “Which is going to leave both public-land duck hunters and even most hunting clubs just completely out of luck.”

Private-land duck-hunting clubs are a popular option for many Arkansas hunters and visiting nonresidents, as even during droughts, most clubs can pump water to create duck habitat and attract birds. The 2026 drought has been so bad, however, that many clubs won’t even have the water to pump.

This is especially troubling to some public-land waterfowl hunters, who worry that the private hunting clubs that can still pump water may end up attracting what few ducks do make it to Arkansas. This could result in a season that benefits the haves over the have-nots.

“I think hunting clubs are the only thing that’s going to make Arkansas appealing for ducks right now,” Spears says. “Most of the smaller rivers, sloughs, and backwaters we have on public land can be walked across in flip-flops right now. So the clubs that have the option to flood fields and create habitat are where the birds are going to be. But with the water table so low, clubs with the ability to pump will be few and far between, so any ducks that do show up during the season are going to have to be babied quite a bit.”

The fact is that if the drought continues in Arkansas, duck hunters on both public and private land alike are going to have to weigh their priorities. Drought conditions are going to be tough on ducks that migrate through Arkansas, and hunting pressure will make surviving the migration harder even for birds that aren’t killed.

This leaves duck hunters with a choice to make.

“It’s going to come down to responsible management on all sides because if we want to maintain the ducks we have, we have to be careful with them,” Birch says. “There are several components to management. It’s not just hunting and the noise of guns, we must be careful disturbing the ducks in any capacity. Your vehicle is an issue because you’re going to bump ducks going in and out of your hunting area, and you’re going to have to be real careful with how many days you hunt and how many hours you hunt in a day, because you’re going to have to let the birds rest.”

With Arkansas’ duck season lasting a total of 51 days with two relatively short splits, hunters looking to limit out consistently are going to be putting serious pressure on what will already be a limited resource.

So instead of hunting hard, both Birch and Spears recommend self-imposed bag limits and time on the water so that the resource can continue to both be conserved and shared.

“My suggestion to any duck hunter in Arkansas is that if you have ducks on your property, take care of them,” says Spears. “With the limited resources, they aren’t going to be able to handle a lot of pressure. You’re going to have to limit how much you hunt and how many birds you shoot, and not be afraid to cut your hunt short. Get a few birds and then leave so the ducks can get back to the water to feed.”

Read Next: Has Hot Cropping Ruined Duck Hunting? Here’s What the Data and Biologists Say About How Flooded Corn Affects Waterfowl Migration

While there is hope that conditions will change, the fact is that the whole of Arkansas’ 2026 duck season may be a nonstarter for many hunters. That short-term pain, however, could help future duck seasons look a little brighter. Spears and Birch are hopeful for a future where water and the waterfowl will eventually return, filling the skies and waterways like flowing veins and once again making Arkansas the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway itself.

“We need to be good stewards of the resource,” urges Spears. “Hunters should do everything they can to keep [ducks] around and not shoot them off what water we do have.”

#8216. Weekend Warm-Up: Hurricane Chase

This episode of the storm-chasing TV series Storm Rising is titled “High Plains Insanity.” Produced by National Geographic, it follows Reed Timmer and Mike Theiss as they attempt to navigate and document an increasingly unpredictable storm season.

The storm-chasing segments are filmed like an action movie but, as Timmer makes clear, thrills are not the primary objective. As the climate changes and disrupts weather patterns, tornadoes are no longer limited to traditional seasons and ranges.

“That’s why we need to be on the ground directly in the path of these tornadoes,” Timmer explains, “to record data that no one else is getting.”

Driving into a potential tornado in Montana. Photo: Screenshot

With hurricane and tornado seasons now overlapping, the duo is forced to split up to cover the widest possible area and collect the most data. For the tornadoes, this means deploying a probe into the funnel.

Up north in the High Plains of the U.S. West and Midwest, Timmer and his dog Gizmo narrowly miss storm after storm. Gizmo is better company but is a very poor navigator, while Timmer isn’t much better. He constantly makes wrong turns as he tries to navigate, drive, and prepare equipment. He does take the time to explain the basic weather science behind what he is failing to catch, such as the conditions that created the baseball-sized hail pummeling his windshield.

Timmer and Gizmo drive through the aftermath of a powerful tornado in the Black Hills of North Dakota. Photo: Screenshot

In Florida, Theiss sleeps in his car to maintain a constant vigil along the coast. Our current scientific abilities, he explains, allow us to track the movement of hurricane systems with decent accuracy. But predicting their strength is much more difficult. Systems can suddenly turn deadly or fizzle out with equally short notice. In this case, the terrible-looking storm comes to nothing. He heads back up north to rejoin Timmer and Gizmo.

Gizmo buffeted by fierce winds. Photo: Screenshot

The reunited trio prepares for a final effort, complete with a windshield-replacement sequence. They immediately drive into the gathering storm, passing through a hail core which, of course, destroys the new windshield. The Lord giveth and taketh away, et cetera. Soon they’re racing on a near-empty tank, as the powerful storm threatens to touch down at any moment.

It’s their last chance this season to deploy the probe. When, after a long, tense chase, they actually manage to do it, both erupt into cheers. Gizmo appears unmoved, despite the fact that this ground probe, deployed in a storm of this type, in this area, has never been deployed before and is sure to yield valuable scientific data.

At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8214. Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Filling Your Tag After Opening Day

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 5:30 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

These days, a buck can’t grow to trophy proportions on hard-hunted public lands by folding up under the pressure exerted by the opening-day crush of hunters. A whitetail lives the 3½ years necessary to grow a bragging set of antlers by learning to cope with the annual influx of hunters during the season’s first days. Every opening day that the buck survives increases his chance of surviving the ensuing openers, as well.

Some big bucks live in locations that allow them to avoid the opening weekend crowd by slipping into the sanctuary offered by state parks, game refuges or private posted land. Others crawl into the most inaccessible and gosh-awful cover their “territory” has to offer — the kind of place hunters walk around, rather than through — and stay there until the crowds leave. And on more than one occasion, I have found a bruiser buck in a tiny, secluded patch of isolated cover — the kind of place most hunters never think to look in.

But the whitetail is a homebody at heart, and he is most comfortable on his loosely defined home turf. You can bet that once the steady crack of opening day rifles slackens to only an occasional pop, those big bucks will be right back to business as usual. And that is the precise reason why your best chance for bagging a wall-hanger occurs after the opening day madness has subsided.

Where you begin your search for that after-the-opener buck hinges upon one factor: the rut. Many states plan their firearms seasons to coincide with the rut, offering their hunters every opportunity to harvest the surplus bucks. Other states, bound in tradition, open the season after the main rut has ended. If you are planning to travel out of your home state to hunt whitetails, it makes sense to hunt a state where the season coincides with the rut, all other criteria being equal.

A mature whitetail buck is a different character during the rut than he is after it. A buck’s sole purpose at that time is to procreate, and this he does with obvious enthusiasm and the stamina of a teenager. There are stories abounding in every deer camp in the country of the crazy antics performed by bucks under the spell of the “rutting moon,” such as lovesick bucks strolling across open fields in broad daylight. Others tell tales of bucks putting their noses to does’ tracks and following those tracks, even after being shot at and missed several times. Rut-crazed bucks, while under the influence of a powerful rush of hormones, have even been known to either pick fights with or attempt to mate with assorted farm livestock.

So rampant are the stories that I’m afraid there exists a whole contingent of deer hunters laboring under the false assumption that all a hunter needs do to down one of these crazy buggers is to walk into the woods, lean up against a stump and wait for one of the poor, sex-addled dummies to saunter on by. Although such tactics work just often enough to keep the myth perpetuated, plenty of hunters have been sitting against stumps for more unsuccessful years than they will probably admit, and they’re still waiting for a buck to wander by.

Hunting whitetails during the rut and after the opening weekend of the season isn’t easy. You must employ all of the skill and woods savvy you have. Any breaks you receive because of a buck’s overactive glands should be considered a bonus.

After the opener, I refine my hunting strategy for big bucks by determining what stage the rut is in. This is important because the stage of the rut will determine where I concentrate most of my midweek hunting efforts.

The whitetail buck is ready to breed long before the first doe enters estrus. During this period, the buck runs off excess energy by beating up saplings, sparring with bushes and, on occasion, duking it out with other bucks. In the process of making rubs on bushes and trees, the buck deposits scents that tell the rest of the whitetail community who was there.

Scrapes begin to show up in increasing numbers as the level of testosterone builds in the buck’s system. The buck paws out scrapes and leaves his scent both in the bare earth of the scrapes and on overhanging branches above the scrapes. Again, this is a means of communicating with other whitetails. The message to does is that the buck is now standing at stud, and it tells other bucks that they had best move along if they know what’s good for them.

A buck will paw out most of his scrapes in areas where they are most likely to be seen or smelled by does. Openings in woods, old logging roads and the edges of woodlots are common places to encounter scrapes. A buck may make a dozen or more scrapes, and he’ll check some of them daily, making sure they are free of debris and liberally doused with musk/urine. These are the scrapes to hunt over.

However, once the does begin cycling into heat, the dominant bucks that handle the bulk of the breeding chores are often so busy accompanying, tracking and servicing does that they neither have the time nor the need to freshen their scrapes. But if you find a string of scrapes that have not been freshened for several days, it doesn’t necessarily mean that the rut is over or that the buck that made the scrapes is hanging from someone else’s game pole. It may mean that the buck is out doing his job.

The best way to determine what stage the rut is in is to spend a day or two in the woods prior to the hunt. If fresh scrapes abound, you will be hunting the pre-coital period, and a stand near a scrape line is going to be hard to beat. If, on the other hand, you find that more than half of the scrapes are old and abandoned, it’s a good bet that the big bucks are out taking care of the does, while the lesser bucks are still hanging around their scrapes, waiting.

If you just don’t have time to get into the woods prior to the season, you can get a good idea about the stage of the rut by speaking with area game managers, milk haulers, rural school bus drivers, postal carriers, loggers, trappers, farmers, grouse hunters, bowhunters or others who spend most of their time in deer country. If the bucks are chasing the does, chances are these people will have observed some of the activity.

Why all of the fuss over determining what stage the rut is in? Because it makes a big difference when you have to decide how you want to hunt after the opening weekend of the season.

When the season begins, if the actual breeding season has not yet begun but bucks are busy pawing out new scrapes and freshening old ones, chances are the bucks will curtail this activity during the high-pressure days at the beginning of the season, resuming their rounds once the hunters thin out. I like to have at least a half-dozen scrape lines pinpointed prior to the opener, and I check them after the opening weekend for any sign of activity. I then concentrate my hunting efforts near the “hottest” scrapes.

If your pre-season scouting and snooping indicates that the actual breeding season is in progress, then hunting over scrapes may not be your best option for tagging a really good buck. Remember, it is often the big, dominant bucks in the area that handle the bulk of the breeding chores. Even in an area with an excellent buck-to-doe ratio, a breeding buck can be kept so busy that he has little time or need to tend his scrapes. Often, a buck will spend 24 hours with the same doe, breeding her several times before her cycle ends. At this time, it is the doe, not the buck, that determines where the pair will travel. Though I would not totally rule out hunting in the vicinity of inactive scrapes at this stage of the rut, there are other options that I’ve found give me a better chance at tagging a big buck.

One of my favorite methods during this period is to concentrate my hunting efforts in areas where I have encountered good numbers of does in the past. Does and fawns tend to hang around together, and they routinely congregate in specific areas, often bedding as a loosely knit group in a favorite swale or sunny hillside. I can’t direct you to these “ladies-only bedrooms,” but I’ll bet there is such a place in the chunk of woods you hunt.

You can locate these places that hold special favor with the does by simply noting those areas where you encounter the most doe activity. After a few years of hunting the same ground, a familiar pattern becomes apparent.

On several occasions, I have located a hotbed of doe activity by simply listening to other hunters. When you hear a hunter complain that he sat on his stand and saw 15 does but not a single buck, find out where he was sitting. You can bet that when the time is right, there will be a buck or two sniffing around the vicinity.

A dominant buck ranges well out of his home area during the actual breeding season. I don’t think he goes out of his way looking for does so much as he is drawn out of the territory when following the tracks of a doe that has visited his scrapes or just wandered through the area. A buck engaged in this activity moves with a single-minded purpose, shuffling along, nose to the ground, seldom looking up, intent upon finding the maker of the tracks he is following. I once missed a buck that trailed a doe past my tree stand in this manner, my arrow slicing harmlessly over the buck’s back only to skitter noisily through the carpet of dry oak leaves a few yards beyond. The buck lifted his head and looked in the direction of the commotion. Apparently satisfied that whatever had made the racket was not what he was looking for, he then ambled on his way while I frantically fumbled around trying to nock another arrow.

When a buck finally tracks down a doe, as often as not, she will then lead the buck on a little chase that, in all likelihood, will take the buck still farther from his home base. By the time the buck actually mates with the doe, he may be miles from his original starting point. On the way back to his core area, it is not unlikely that the buck will cut the track of another “hot” doe and repeat the scenario.

All of this rambling makes it impossible to pattern a buck during the height of the breeding season, prompting many knowledgeable whitetail hunters to claim that taking a good buck during the peak of the rut is mostly a matter of luck. I disagree.

Hunting near a known “doe hangout” can help. And you can stack the deck in your favor even more by hunting a funnel area near a concentration of does. A funnel is simply any natural or man-made feature of the terrain that restricts the movement of deer. An hourglass-shaped section of woods is the classic funnel. Deer moving from one wide portion of the hourglass to the other must pass through the narrow waist. In farm country, the funnel may be a brushy fenceline connecting two woodlots. In the rugged country of the West, where whitetails are becoming increasingly common, a jutting jaw of sheer rimrock can force whitetails into a confined passage. Funnels work well because even though a buck with a snootful of “hot” doe scent wouldn’t care if he was standing in the middle of Madison Avenue, the does do not lose their heads. Does will stick to cover when on the move, even when there is a grunting, panting Romeo dogging their every move.

In many whitetail states, hunters are left with no choice but to hunt well after the main rutting period is over. At first, hunting after the opening weekend under these circumstances appears to be a futile endeavor. Without the promise of the rut to keep the bucks on the move, and with the absence of hunters to jar deer loose, what chance does the midweek hunter have of scoring on a decent buck? He has a pretty good one, I would say.

First, although the bulk of the breeding chores are completed in about a two-week period, there is still some activity after the main rut is over. Does that were not impregnated will cycle back into heat 28 days after their first estrus cycle ends. In some states, this is just about the time the rifle season rolls around. In addition, in some of the best whitetail areas, it is not uncommon for female fawns of the year to breed. This often applies to as many as 40 percent of them. Frequently, these young breeders experience their first estrus cycle later than older does. So, don’t strike out the possibility of the rut working in your favor even though the peak has passed. In that part of Minnesota where I live, the bulk of the breeding is completed by mid-November, yet each year, I find active scrapes late into November and even December. Last season, during an especially mild December, I found a pair of bushel-basket-size recently freshened scrapes while hunting grouse on New Year’s Eve day. If you can find these “late scrapes,” concentrate your hunting efforts near them.

But even if you fail to find any of these scrapes later in the year, you still stand a good chance of tagging a buck during the middle of the week if you let the after-effects of the rut work in your favor. During the rut, a buck takes little time out to eat. By the end of the breeding season, a buck that began the rut in peak condition can be drawn and thin, actually haggard looking. Some bucks lose up to 20 percent of their body weight during the course of the rut. Across the Northern states and Canada, where winter-kill is an annual problem, the big, breeding bucks follow the fawns in death each winter simply because they run off all of their reserve fat during the rut and are often unable to replenish it before the onset of winter snows.

Although the largest bucks are often nocturnal in their feeding habits at other times of the year, when the rut is over, dominant bucks cannot afford to limit their foraging to the hours of darkness alone. In order to regain their strength and put on the layer of fat necessary to see them through the winter, they must forage during the day. This gives the after-the-opener hunter the chance he needs to find bucks up and about during shooting hours.

This need to feed heavily does not necessarily mean that a big buck will be found sauntering around an alfalfa field at high noon, scarfing down the last frost-tinged greens of the year. It does mean that rather than tucking himself safely away in a thicket long before the morning sun ever peeks over the ridge, a buck will now tend to linger at the feeding grounds longer in the morning. Does and fawns often feed for an hour after sunrise, and now, bucks will begin to do the same. In the evening, when they normally would not begin feeding until complete darkness had fallen, bucks will now arrive at the feeding sites earlier — often in that last precious minute of shooting light.

Even at midday, the big boys will not be content to simply lay in their beds and chew their cuds. I have noticed that once the rut is over, bucks are much more likely to rise from their beds and spend an hour or two at midday — often between 11 a.m. and 2 p.m. — browsing near their bedding sites.

All of this daylight activity is a big plus for the after-the-opener hunter who must depend upon natural deer movements, rather than other hunters, to put a buck in front of him. The natural inclination is to hunt from a stand near the feeding area, but this is a mistake when you’re after mature bucks. A big buck is constantly alert, using each of his senses to detect the slightest hint of danger. Never is a buck more alert than when he is feeding during shooting light because he knows he is most vulnerable then. The bucks I have observed feeding during the season have been wound-up tighter than a watch spring and ready to snap at the slightest provocation. One miscue on your part, whether it is movement or scent or whatever, will send him running. I try to avoid known feeding areas, and I concentrate my efforts near the bedding areas.

Hunting near the bedding area means that any shot I take will most often be attempted in good light. That’s important for a clean kill. A buck may not arrive back at his bedding area until the sun is an hour high in the morning, and he may leave his bed to begin a dawdling trip to the feeding area in late afternoon. Instead of risking a poor shot at a shadowy form in the gray gloom of dusk or dawn, I take my shot when the light is in my favor.

Also, by concentrating my efforts in the vicinity of the buck’s daytime haunts, I have a better chance of catching him up and about during the midday hours. If I am forced to move into the bedding area and take a stand at mid-morning, I risk detection by the buck. But if I move into my stand in the dark and set up quietly, I double my chances of scoring. If I fail to take the buck as he returns from breakfast, I will be there waiting when he gets up for lunch or dinner.

But you don’t have to confine yourself to a stand. If conditions allow for quiet stillhunting, I like nothing better than to either prowl the edges of a buck’s daytime haunts or simply tread my way along a ridge littered with the acorns of the white oak, moving only a few yards at a time and stopping often to look, listen and feel. Often after the crowds of opening day are gone from the woods for another year, it is just me and the deer — one-on-one. And that’s the way I like it best.

This story, “After Opening Day,” appeared in the November 1987 issue of Outdoor Life. Read more from this series:

#8212. American Climber Dies at 5,800m After Summiting Mera Peak

Richard Carlos Skidmore, 66, of the United States, was found dead in his tent on Mera Peak on Sunday, November 22, after summiting the 6,476m mountain the previous morning.

According to Solukhumbu district police, he reached the summit at approximately 10am on Saturday, November 21, and descended to the high camp at around 5,800m, where he spent the night.

He was found dead the next morning. Past Mera Peak climbers have suggested on social media that it was unusual to overnight in the high camp after summiting so early in the day.

His body was evacuated by helicopter to Kathmandu for an autopsy. The cause of death remains undetermined. An initial report raised the possibility of altitude-related illness or a cardiac event, but neither has been established medically.

Skidmore previously summited Lobuche Peak in 2024, according to the Everest Chronicle and the Italian Alpine Club’s publication Lo Scarpone. So he had previous Himalayan climbing experience above 6,000m.

The Himalayan Database does include Mera Peak, but its coverage of popular trekking peaks is selective. However, it notes that since 2018, four people have died on Mera Peak, not counting Skidmore.

#8210. Here’s Proof That You Don’t Need to Break the Bank for a Hardside Deer Hunting Blind

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 10:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Growing up, I never really hunted out of a blind. Here in the Northeast, we traditionally hunt from treestands or just pick a good spot on the ground with natural cover. Sure, I watched plenty of hunting shows where they sat in a hardsided blind over a food plot, but that just wasn’t an option for me. That is, until recently, when I started planting my own food plots here in Pennsylvania.

Once I figured out the planting cycle, I knew it was time to get my hands on a hardsided blind. But the absurd prices of some of the higher-end models really turned my stomach. Then, I heard about the Maverick XL-V and knew it’d be a great fit for my new food plot.

This six-panel blind provides a near 360 degree view with large, vertical windows that are perfect for archery hunting. It’s also quite spacious, easily allowing two people to sit and hunt comfortably. But the best part is, it’s a fraction of the price that you’d pay for some of the competition out there. Here’s what you need to know about this versatile hunting blind.

After tending to my food plot for the better part of the summer, the clover was flourishing and my Maverick XL-V had arrived just in time to build it a month or so ahead of the season. This allowed the deer to get used to the blind’s presence prior to the season getting underway in October.

The first true test of the blind was setting it up. Maverick talked up its six-panel lock-in system, and I was excited to see how it all came together. Unfortunately, the first shipment I received was missing the door panel. After a quick call to customer support, the missing door panel arrived about a week later, but that panel was missing the window. Rather than wait any longer, I opted to simply replace the window with a piece of plexiglass cut to fit.

The good news is that the lock-in system works flawlessly. The panels all fit together quickly and seamlessly, making it easy to get the blind placed on our homemade platform with just two people. (For some of the bigger, spendier hard-sided blinds, you need a tractor or forklift.) Once it was set up, all that was left was to wait for our archery season to open.

On the opening weekend of archery, my girlfriend and I had an amazing first evening sitting in the XL-V blind. With several does and a few small bucks under 20 yards away, they never had any idea we were there. It was spacious enough for two blind chairs, our hunting packs, and our bows to fit with plenty of room to spare.

Since then, I’ve spent several weeks in and out of the blind, with deer in the plot almost every time. I have yet to see a target buck arrive in daylight, but I have a feeling the skunk will be knocked out of the blind in no time.

After spending several weeks in the XL-V, I can say I’ve grown quite fond of this blind. The windows offer a great view and operate on friction hinges, with three easy-to-use latches to keep them shut. Thus far, the windows open and close silently, which is obviously incredibly important to avoid spooking any deer in range.

The door offers two easy-to-use handles, too, and is large enough to fit through without having to contort my body to squeeze into the blind. Aside from the missing components with delivery, the set up was easy and straightforward. The panels with the lock-in system were intuitive and functioned as expected. I honestly expected to struggle getting it set up on our homemade platform, but was pleasantly surprised to be proven wrong.

Obviously, the issues I experienced with the missing door and then the missing window were not ideal. It’s hard to say if that’s a one-off and I simply got unlucky, or if there’s a larger issue at play there. Aside from that, there are a few other areas I think the XL-V could improve.

First, the near-360 degree view is great, but it leaves you backlit on sunny days. I’d love to see included covers for the windows behind you to avoid the backlighting issue. On top of that, this blind is not airtight, meaning you need to be aware of where your scent will be going just like hunting from a treestand.

While the XL-V might have some room for improvements, it’s worth keeping in mind how affordable this hardsided blind is compared to others on the market. It might lack some of the high-end features those blinds offer, but it still gets the job done for a fraction of the price.

Once getting it set up and deployed in the plot, I’ve thoroughly enjoyed every sit. It’s provided me with some incredible hunts, plenty of room to stretch my legs throughout some long sits, and ensure I have all of the gear I need. If you’re in the market for a budget hunting blind, don’t overlook the Maverick XL-V hunting blind.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

#8208. New Route on Classic Tien Shan Peak

Three Polish climbers have established a new line on the north face of one of the Tien Shan’s best-known climbing peaks.

Michal Makowski, Robert Paluch, and Rafal Zając took 11 hours to climb their new 850m line on 4,777m Free Korea Peak, in Kyrgyzstan’s northern Tien Shan Mountains. They then spent a further 11 hours returning to base camp.

They named the route The Polish Barber, after pioneer American climber Henry Barber, who soloed the wall in 1976. Their line shares the first 250–300m with Barber’s, Makowski told ExplorersWeb.

“According to the information I have, [the remaining 600-650m] was previously unclimbed and had never been attempted by another team,” said Makowski. “We reached the summit ridge and then began rappelling down from there.

Corona Hut, the team’s base camp. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Free Korea Peak — Pik Svobodnaya Koreya in Russian — was already famous among mountaineers in the former Soviet Union because of its 900m north wall. in 2018, Russian climber Valery Balesin suggested that it was no longer possible to find new lines on this well-known face, although advances in steep ice-climbing techniques have clearly opened new possibilities.

“My friends and I researched Free Korea Peak and realized that part of the north face still has unclimbed terrain,” said Makowski.

On the Polish Barber route. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Their route was almost completely ice climbing. The crux came midway up the wall, when they faced a particularly steep section of ice that they graded at Alpine Ice 4+. The rest of the route was 3/3+. Fortunately, “protection on the Polish Barber was really good, except for the last 150m, where ice was pretty thin and crusty,” said Makowski.

Climbing by moonlight. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Despite its former popularity, they had the valley almost to themselves during their week in camp. There was just one Russian group doing a tourist trek up nearby Corona Peak.

A scrambly section on Free Korea Peak. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

#8207. The Hunt for the Jameson Buck, a 250-Inch Kansas Legend That Lived in an Old Strip Mine

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 1:41 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

This story, “Ghost Buck,” appeared in the November 1991 issue of Outdoor Life. The Bruce Jameson buck ultimately scored 248 1/8 inches after deductions, according to Boone and Crockett. Although it was one of the top-scoring archery bucks of all time when it was killed, it’s listed as the 16th largest nontypical ever taken in Kansas. Though the story mentions Pope and Young, Jameson’s name isn’t listed among its records. The rack is now owned by Bass Pro Shops.

The big whitetail moved steadily, head lowered, nosing a course through a lunarlike landscape of earth mounds, dark puddles and hair-thick brush. Strip mining had turned the area raw and made hunting supremely difficult. Yet this giant of a buck moved on. Something compelled him, an urge perhaps. It was mid-November, the peak of the rut.

Then abruptly the buck froze, as if gripped by an old instinct for caution. From atop a rise, he surveyed the area.

To bowhunter Bruce Jameson, who knelt against the base of a cottonwood 50 yards away, it was now clear that all of the speculation about the buck had been true. Somehow the hundreds of hunters before him who had failed to get a close look at this ghost buck had emerged with an accurate picture — perhaps because it was nearly impossible to exaggerate in describing the animal. Everything about the buck was massive. His long, heavy body seemed to melt into a neck that was a barrel of solid muscle from supporting his towering, forearm-thick antlers. The bowhunter clutched his compound Pearson Spoiler and saw his knuckles turn a splotchy white.

This buck, so well-known but seldom seen, had drawn Jameson into the woods 20 of the past 21 mornings. He had been taunted by a combination of too much distance and brush. Day after day, there had been no clear shot. Still, the bowhunter felt a kind of jubila­tion as he watched the world-class whitetail lower his head and fade into the brush. After all, he had just accomplished something no other hunter had — he had seen the ghost buck while hunting.

At the time, the walls of Jameson’s Pittsburg, Kan­sas, bowhunting and taxidermy shop were lined with big-game mounts. Most were whitetail bucks of envi­able size. Still, to a mount, they were overshadowed by one buck in particular, which, once noticed, instantly took your eyes away from the rest. Hunters had come from all parts of southeastern Kansas to marvel at this huge non-typical buck. His main beams were nearly as thick as a softball where they rose from the mount, and they thickened as they climbed into a crown of spike­ like points. When displayed at a trophy deer show, people had crowded around, labeling it the most im­pressive non-typical to come from a state that’s been dominating the record books for a quarter-century.

Equally provocative was the fact that the deer was still in the wild. The display cape was that of another buck. The gargantuan antlers had been found after the ghost buck had shed them the previous winter. It wasn’t the first time, either.

Deer hunters around town got their first hint of an exceptional horned buck when John Jameson, Bruce’s father, happened upon a shed antler during a 1987 bowhunt. Though bleached from the sun and pocked by rodents, there was no mistaking that it came from a trophy buck. The single antler was unusually massive and sported eight points.

Local hunters were surprised that no one had ever seen the buck that carried it. Many speculated that the beast had already died a natural death. The following spring though, Jameson’s nephew, Doug Kratz, discovered that the ghost was still in their presence when he happened across a massive, freshly shed antler. A few weeks later the other side, an eye­ opening set, also turned up. Together the two antlers carried a total of 22 points.

The presence of the big buck in the Pittsburg area was particularly exciting to the Jamesons. Bruce Jameson is one of 10 children who grew up hunting and fishing with their father. In fact, all of the Jamesons, including their father, still bow hunt together regularly. Yet, the Jameson’s home range, southeastern Kansas, is the only area of the state not known for producing exceptional whitetail racks. Hunting pressure is high and the habitat fair at best for producing big bucks.

Like scores of other hunters, Jameson planned on hunting the ghost buck hard during the 1988 season, but his new taxidermy and bowhunting shop was gaining popularity with local hunters. He couldn’t get away.

Jameson was determined, however, to keep his ears open while he manned his Main Street shop. The progressing season started out as a repeat of the past: no sightings, and no signs of a huge whitetail — no big tracks, massive rubs or huge scrapes. Still, the attraction of this buck, and his mystique, seemed to build with each passing week.

There probably isn’t a deer camp or small hunting town in America that doesn’t have a legendary buck — an animal so massive, so elu­sive, so remarked upon, that his image is forever associated with something beyond the ordinary. The ghost buck is an idea more than a form, and as such, it has a special invincibil­ity. Yet Bruce Jameson was determined to change that.

Luck helped. Soon, Jameson’s good friend Dennis Scales accomplished something that instantly made him a local celebrity — he glimpsed the ghost buck. It was still early in the morning when Stales spotted a doe and a small buck feeding across a field of milo stubble.

From his truck, Scales could easily watch as the unimpressive pair fed across the expanse of knee-high cover. Then it happened. Scales, an avid deer hunter, watched in wonder as the big non-typical rose from the scant cover and hurried toward the little buck and doe. As soon as the monster buck reached the doe he in­stantly flattened out, totally out of sight in what looked like open cover. It was a ritual that Scales witnessed three times before the passing of a distant pickup truck spooked the big whitetail into the woods. No one else saw the famous buck during the gun or bow season.

Those who’ve driven the southeastern Kansas area can understand the amazement sur­rounding the notion of a giant buck in flatlands country. With a decided absence of wilder­ ness, every mile of land is evenly gridded by gravel roads that are often traveled by small­ game hunters and fishermen, farmers and com­muters.

Despite Scales’ sighting, and the discovery of another huge shed antler, Jameson was not encouraged. “My heart would start pounding when I would look up and think of myself in a tree with him coming down the trail,” he mused. “But I knew it was going to be tough to pick one buck, a buck that you know is at least 5 1/2 years old, in an area with heavy pressure and heavy cover. I also knew that I’d hunted this area all of my life and never seen the buck. I couldn’t help but occasionally wonder if he was still alive.”

One summer evening, Jameson found out. “I do quite a bit of looking in August,” he said. “The bucks will be hanging together, and because they haven’t been pressured you can sometimes catch them coming out to feed in the evenings.”

Along with a cousin, Bruce drove to a soybean field in his hunting area. Most sum­mertime bucks are calm and tolerant of distant vehicles, but one buck in particular melted into the nearby woods when Jameson was still 150 yards away. That was close enough.

“I didn’t need binoculars to know it was him,” Jameson recalled. “I was in a state of awe. I just turned to my cousin and said, ‘You have no idea what you just saw — he’s one of the best in the state, probably one of the best in the nation.’ It was good to know that I’d at least get a chance to hunt him.”

Jameson also knew that he wouldn’t be alone in his quest. The lad who’d found the most recent sheds had disclosed the buck’s location. Going into the season about a dozen bowhunters had sworn to hold out for this buck, which seemed now more flesh than ghost. In the weeks before the opening of deer season, a continuous cloud of dust hung over the dirt roads as hunters and non-hunters alike cruised along, hoping for a glimpse of the legendary buck. Then on October 1, bowhunters swarmed into the woods, but again it was as if the buck had turned to mist.

Meanwhile, Jameson bided his time, hop­ing the upcoming rut would draw the buck out. Though Jameson had a good idea where the deer lived, he couldn’t get permission to enter the property. Instead, he set up 1 1/2 miles away and waited.

“I figured that once the rut started he’d start covering more ground,” Jameson rea­soned. “Even then I knew he’d be tough be­ cause of the terrain.”

Deer hunting in this part of Kansas can be, well, the pits. This is coal country. In previous decades huge chunks of ground were clawed open to get at coal deposits. The strip-mined areas look like moon surface.

Piles of dirt from eight to 30 feet high lay interspaced with water-filled pits. Where there is dirt there is dense brush. The jungle — and swamplike strip pits offer some of the most difficult hunting in the nation.

Jameson spent his first week in a tree at the edge of “the pits” near a slough that would offer the buck the easiest and most direct route through the area. He saw only a small buck and some fawns.

Frustrated, he moved to an area that held a small green wheat field and a scattering of hedge trees. Though he sighted and passed up a number of lesser bucks, there was no sign of the ghost buck. The unseasonably warm and dry weather that ushered in the November rut­ — hunters in the area saw more mosquitoes than snowflakes — further tilted the odds against Jameson.

Finally, Jameson got a much needed break in the form of a light shower, which would allow him to pick up tracks. He started slowly traveling the back roads.

“We often look for what we call ‘shortcut’ trails in the dust on the shoulders of roads,” Jameson explained. “They’re basically trails that big bucks use to cover a lot of ground and to get from section to section without being seen.”

The veteran hunter observed that these shortcut trails seem to be utilized only by big bucks, seldom by does or lesser bucks. “You’ll usually only find the tracks of one or two bucks,” Jameson added. “We check the road edges because the trails are tough to see or follow in the woods, they get used so little.”

A few years earlier, in just such a place, one of his brothers had arrowed a trophy with a Boone and Crockett Club non-typical score in excess of 200 points.

Jameson soon found tracks coming and going from the section he’d hunted earlier in the season. There were two sets of buck tracks: one big, the other less so. Experience told the hunter that the larger set probably belonged to a dark-racked buck he’d seen and passed up near the wheat field.

But what about the other tracks? They weren’t overly impressive, and he couldn’t remember seeing them before. But it was their location, not their size, that excited Jameson. It was a prime spot for an old, wary buck to travel. “Maybe,” he thought, “just maybe …”

Fired by a hunch, Jameson took a rare morning off from his treestand vigil and planned to move from his wheat field location to the slough at the edge of the pits where he’d first started.

Jameson had just started the walk into the slough when something caught his eye. “I looked across the field and there he was,” said Jameson. “I could tell it was him no problem. He had his nose down bird-dogging it back and forth. He was looking for a doe.”

Jameson’s hunch was confirmed; the buck had gone right through the slough where he’d wanted his stand. But rather than bemoan his bad timing, Jameson decided to make the best of the situation as he watched the non-typical disappear into the timber.

“A lot of hunters make the mistake of seeing a big buck and then not doing anything about it,” noted Jameson. “We’ve taken sev­eral trophy-class bucks by swinging around and getting ahead of them. Sometimes it works, sometimes it doesn’t, but I figured it was worth a try.”

The buck had been heading north, possibly toward the shortcut trail Jameson had found a few days earlier. The bowhunter wasted no time circling ahead of the buck. He ran down the trail that bisected the two sections, jumped a fence, and sprinted to the top of one of the many dirt mounds.

It was a guess — hoping the buck would cross 30 yards to his right — but it was Jameson’s only chance. Even if the buck came his way he knew that the thick underbrush would make a shot next to impossible.

Jameson had been in place only five min­utes when he caught movement. It was the ghost buck, passing at a steady clip. The range was too long and the brush too thick. Jameson’s heart was drumming against his rib cage. He tried to sneak a few steps closer. but he couldn’t get within 50 yards.

Then, for reasons unclear to the hunter. the whitetail suddenly stopped and raised his head. In the next moment, he was gone. disappearing over the edge of a mound. Jameson found himself staring at the scene as it was just minutes ago — that is, without the ghost buck­ — and he felt a kind of pleasurable sadness. “It was a strange feeling.” he remembered. “I’d actually figured out where he’d go, but I was also disappointed. You simply don’t get many chances at a deer like him. He sure had proven that over the past several seasons. I had to wonder whether I’d ever get another chance.”

Feeling almost electrified, Jameson waited a few minutes before sneaking back carefully to the spot where he”d last seen the whitetail. At least, he reasoned, he could get a look at the tracks and perhaps figure out once again where the buck was heading.

When he saw them, he instantly recognized the tracks — they were the smaller set he’d seen on the shortcut trail. Jameson walked in the direction the buck had taken and paused at the top of the next mound, wondering why he hadn’t seen the deer cresting one of the sea of little ridges.

Without warning, there was a loud crash. Jameson twisted around and saw the buck of his dreams jump from a hiding place 35 yards away, staying to a course that kept distance between him and the bowhunter. Instinct took over. It seemed Jameson’s hundreds of hours of indoor and outdoor shooting had somehow led him to this shot — he was ready. “I drew, got ahead of the buck, and when the time was right, I released the arrow without actually thinking about it.”

Jameson watched his Anderson 365 mag­num broadhead enter the buck as the animal stepped through a tiny clearing. Ironically. it was one of the rare occasions when this sharp­ shooting bowhunter had been unsure of his arrow placement. He watched the buck pause briefly atop the next mound, then move out of sight as if not seriously wounded.

At the thought of a bad hit on such a magnificent animal, Jameson fell to his knees and was sick. Long a man of deep conviction, he did what came naturally in a time of crisis — he said a quick prayer for the animal’s fast and humane death. “I call upon God anytime I feel I need help.” he said. Then he thought to go get his father, who was waiting nearby.

An hour later Jameson, his father, John, and his brother Richard were back in the area. The trio quietly surveyed the scene of the shot. There was blood. They soon found the arrow. but it was floating in a nearby pit of water, wave-cleansed of any tissue or hair.

After deciding the trail looked good, the trio inched in the direction the ghost buck had taken. They saw that the buck had stopped and changed directions. The father and sons found the huge whitetail at the top of the next dump­ — barely 70 yards away from where it had been hit. Clearly, the ghost buck had succumbed within a matter of a few seconds.

“When he saw the deer,” Jameson re­called of his father, “tears came to his eyes and he hugged me around the neck. He’s always had a great sense of pride in each of his six sons and our accomplishments. I’ll never forget it.”

What will forever be known as the “Jameson buck” will go down as one of the most impres­sive whitetail bucks of all time. The mass of the rack was almost indescribable and, along with the skull plate, weighed an eye-opening 12 pounds.

After the 60-day drying period, the mam­moth rack scored 253 6/8 Pope and Young Club points — the tally may well go higher when scored by the club’s official panel. As it stands, the “Jameson buck” is the third largest non­ typical whitetail arrowed in history.

Read Next: I Shot the Third Biggest Elk of All Time

A close look at the ghost buck revealed how he had lived virtually undetected by sportsmen for years. Though he field-dressed at 243 pounds, his feet sported surprisingly small hoofs. Jameson also theorized that the jungle of points on the buck’s rack may have kept him from rubbing on big trees.  “When you really start looking at the rack you realize that he couldn’t rub all of his velvet on a big tree,” said the hunter. “He probably had to rely on small trees or maybe even brush. It’s possible we saw his rubs and just dismissed them as those of a small buck.”
