#8205. 💪 Into the High Sierra: ❄️ Snow, Ice & Forrester Pass 🏔️

I packed up from my stealth spot before daybreak and used the park bathroom to change and brush my teeth.

Sitting at a picnic table in the shade, I pulled up my blog. That’s when I realized I had forgotten to hit publish on the post I had scheduled for the previous week. I rushed to get it published, feeling frustrated with myself and the growing pressure to keep up with my writing. Once my blog was published online, I spent the remainder of the morning working on my next entry.

I picked at my leftover Chinese food for breakfast as I sorted through my resupply and packed my backpack for the trail.

Walking through town, I stopped at Carl’s Jr. for a Double Quarter Pounder with Cheese and a strawberry milkshake. I packed the burger away to eat later on the trail.

Before hitching out, I stopped at Elevation Gear, where I finished my ice cream and spent a few minutes visiting with Marble, Electrolyte, and Pickles while telling them my plans.

Walking along the road toward the Horseshoe Meadow turnoff, I dropped my pack to hitch. I stood with my thumb out, hoping for a ride. A few vehicles stopped to offer rides, but they were heading either to Whitney Portal or the Alabama Hills. A couple of other drivers pulled over only to tell me they didn’t have room.

The gestures were kind, but each time a vehicle stopped, my hopes went up. Having them dashed by another apology made the wait even more discouraging.

After 30 minutes with no luck, I grabbed my backpack and walked farther out of town.

A farmer working in a field across the street told me he had been watching me try to hitch a ride down the road. He wished me luck.

The driver, Taquito, told me he was a Triple Crown thru-hiker from Omaha. He was on vacation, hiking and providing trail magic to hikers. We talked about hiking, his Triple Crown experiences, and the trail magic he was sharing with hikers. He took me all the way to the Horseshoe Meadow trailhead.

At the trailhead, Taquito offered me even more trail magic in the form of Gatorade, an apple, and Gummy Clusters. Taking advantage of the nearby trash cans, I drank the Gatorade, ate the apple, and demolished my double burger before heading out on the trail. I tucked the Gummy Clusters away for later.

Leaving the trailhead at 4:30 p.m., I began my hike into the mountains. Lone Pine had been hot during the day, now, in elevation the evening air was getting cold. The loose, sandy trail made walking difficult, especially with my heavy backpack. I moved slowly, tired and a little stressed about finding a place to camp before the elevation started.

I spent too much time looking off-trail for flat spots. Two miles in, the trees began to grow thicker, I suddenly came across a sandy clearing with a rabbit. Seeing it hopping around so carefree made the area feel safe and inviting. I liked the idea of sharing this little clearing with the rabbit, so I decided this was where I would camp.

I used my newly acquired ice axe to scrape and level the sandy ground, making a flat spot for my tent.

For dinner, I made a Frito taco bowl with dehydrated pea-protein “beef” and dried refried beans. It hit the spot after a long day. With a full belly, a newly leveled tent spot, and a rabbit for company, I crawled into my tent. Too tired to write, I fell asleep around 8 p.m. with a bit of daylight still lingering. I had a long way to go, but at least I was back in the mountains.

I woke up at 3 a.m. to a dark, chilly morning. I was camped on the Cottonwood Pass Trail, 2.6 miles from its junction with the PCT at Mile 751.4. Nearly 1,200 feet lower in elevation. I opened one of my tent vestibules to help ventilate the condensation before burrowing back into my quilt for a little more rest.

At 4:15 a.m., I woke to the howling of coyotes. It made me smile. I love being in nature. Still tucked into my quilt, I reached for my headlamp in the top pocket of my backpack, where I always keep it. Although I normally sleep with it around my neck, I knew exactly where to find it.

While getting breakfast ready, I also pulled out my snacks for the day. I try to eat my heavier, bulkier foods first to lighten my pack. As I was packing up, I spotted Spicy Bite (Southern California) hiking toward town. He graciously offered to carry out my trash. Everything was inside a Ziploc bag, including half a family-size bag of Fritos that I had hiked in. I slipped the bag into his backpack without making him take it off. One less thing to carry!

A little after 7 a.m., I was hiking across a green, grassy meadow covered in frost. The sun hadn’t yet risen over the mountains, and the air grew colder as I approached a stream. After crossing it, I looked up at the steep mountainside ahead. I checked my map, trying to figure out where the trail passed through the snow-covered pass above. I began preparing myself for the climb.

The trail entered a pine forest and began to switchback.

The sun began to warm the earth. Chipmunks scurried over the white boulders and fallen logs along the trail. At the last switchback before the top, I stopped for a break. I was above 11,000 feet and beginning to notice a slight headache coming on. After five minutes, I pushed on to the top and finally rejoined the PCT.

Just minutes later, I stopped at the first trickle of flowing water. My headache was slowly developing, and my stomach wasn’t feeling right. I needed to hydrate and give myself time to adjust before continuing farther into the Sierras. I spent the next hour and a half sitting beside the trail at 11,132 feet, drinking water and trying to stay warm. My headache improved slightly, but I still wasn’t feeling quite right.

Pushing to higher elevations can be dangerous. In 2022, I had been just a few days behind a young hiker who lost her life on Forester Pass due to altitude-related issues. That experience had stayed with me and reminded me not to take the mountains lightly.

As I packed up, I noticed another hiker continuing past the Lone Pine exit. It was Diva (Tennessee), who had already built up his trail legs after hiking the Appalachian Trail and then the Florida Trail over the past year. He was planning to continue north to Kearsarge Pass.

We hiked together to the frozen Chicken Spring Lake, where we met a group of local high school students and their teachers. The students were about to graduate and were on a 20-day backpacking trip.

Having company lifted my spirits, even though I still wasn’t feeling 100%. Before leaving the lake, I noticed my feet were feeling swollen and my shoes were getting tight. With the altitude already on my mind, it was another thing I was paying attention to. I took an ibuprofen, collected a little more water, and said goodbye as Diva continued north.

As I climbed over the next pass and entered Kings Canyon National Park, the landscape opened up to beautiful views. A meadow stretched out to my left, while the trail wrapped around the mountainside. To my right, the majestic pink and white rock walls of Cirque Peak towered above me at 12,900 feet.

I stopped for lunch and noticed dark clouds gathering in the distance. I moved more snacks into my hip-belt pocket. With most of my elevation gain behind me for the day, I wasn’t too concerned about being caught in a storm while exposed on a climb, though I did pick up my pace.

As thunder rumbled in the dark clouds, I hurried to the next water source. I found a few flat spots nearby and collected water, letting my gravity filter do its work while I set up my tent. With the storm moving in, I wanted to get settled before it arrived.

Happy with my setup, I was about to put my gear inside when I looked up. Above my tent, I saw one dead tree leaning against another, with yet another dead tree on the opposite side. With thunder sounding closer, I quickly decided to move my tent. I relocated to a completely different spot a short distance away.

Another hiker, Scott (New Zealand), came down the trail. He was planning to continue a few more miles before setting up for the night. I finally threw my gear into my tent just as it started to hail.

Small hailstones continued falling steadily. I occasionally bumped the sides of my tent to knock off the accumulating ice. It was only 5 p.m., but I was happy to be inside and dry, with enough water for the night.

I boiled water and mixed half a Mountain House pepperoni pizza meal with half a chicken alfredo meal.

After the hail stopped, the golden sun came streaming through the pine trees. Tiny white pieces of hail were scattered across the ground, contrasting with the beautiful green landscape.

I brushed my teeth and placed my bear can behind a large fallen tree as the sun dipped below the mountains.

Crawling into my tent, I thought about how far I had come. I was proud of myself for making it this far on the trail and stepping into the high-altitude Sierras. It had taken bravery and strength to venture this far into the wilderness on my own. Even with the cold, I was genuinely happy to be surrounded by such a beautiful place. I felt grateful to be there, with a deep respect for the mountains and the wilderness I am fortunate enough to experience.

At 3:45 a.m., my alarm pulled me out of sleep. I wanted to eat up any food that required cooking today so that tomorrow, when I pushed over Forrester Pass, I could just grab bars and snacks and keep moving.

After boiling water, I made oatmeal and a mix of hot cocoa and coffee.

Part of the reason I struggle to hike with others is that I’m a slower hiker and take longer to get out of camp. I get frustrated when I’m the last one to leave, and that frustration can often affect my attitude throughout the day.

This morning, I realized that taking my time getting out of camp was a form of self-care. When I give myself time to get everything done without rushing, I’m happier and more relaxed. I also know I’ve taken care of everything at camp and haven’t forgotten any chores.

At 7 a.m., I continued my hike. The trail descended toward Rock Creek, where it opened into a beautiful grassy valley surrounded by snow-covered mountains. Water was flowing everywhere. It was a completely different landscape from the desert I’d been hiking through the past two months.

As I approached Rock Creek, I passed established campsites, a few with hikers still packing up. The creek was rushing, and the morning air was cold.

The stepping stones used later in the year were covered in inches of rushing water. Looking downstream, I spotted an old fallen tree with no bark. It would likely be slippery with the frost that morning. A little farther down, I saw a newer-looking fallen tree across the creek. Thinking this might be an option, I started climbing onto it, but some of the bark slipped off, revealing a smooth, slippery surface. Looking farther along the log, I noticed more sections where the bark was missing.

I hiked back upstream, past the trail, but didn’t find any better options for a dry crossing. I felt most comfortable getting my shoes wet, hoping that if I crossed on the rocks, I could at least keep the rest of my clothing dry.

I tried stepping across the rocks first. As soon as both feet were on the slippery rocks, I decided it would be better to just cross the creek. Plunging my legs into the freezing current, the water reached my knees. Even part of my rolled-up pants got wet as I climbed out on the other side.

I met a NOBO JMT hiker named Will (California), who had taken off his shoes to cross the creek and was putting them back on.

At the top of the next hill, I took a break on a boulder with a view, dreaming of French toast with syrup, maybe even peanut butter.

Scott from the day before walked up while I was taking a break. This was when we officially met.

Hiking through a rocky boulder field, I heard black-capped Chickadees chirping something that sounded like the word “cheeseburger.” Every time I heard it, I couldn’t help but laugh and found myself getting hungry!

Reaching Crabtree Meadow, I crossed Whitney Creek using a log. I felt a little uneasy about my balance with my backpack and how high the log was above the creek, but I made it across. I was proud of myself!

After crossing Whitney Creek, I passed the first trail junction leading to Mount Whitney

The PCT climbed through exposed switchbacks with some great views. I stopped halfway up to apply sunscreen.

Passing the second junction to Whitney, I was slightly tempted to take it, but continued hiking the PCT. I didn’t have enough food to add the extra day and a half, and the last forecast I’d read was predicting snow in a few days.

I picked my way through some patchy snow before arriving at Wallace Creek. After hiking up and down the creek looking for a dry crossing, I couldn’t find one. This time I took off my shoes, socks, calf compression sleeves, and gaiters before crossing.

The creek was cold, the rocks were slippery, and the water was high enough to get the bottom of my shorts a little wet.

I dried my feet with a bandanna and let them air out for a bit before putting everything back on.

Clouds started rolling in as the afternoon continued. I needed to push to get as close to Forrester Pass as I could.

As I approached the climb up Big Horn Plateau, the clouds grew closer, and I could hear thunder rumbling. Seeing a cluster of trees about 300 feet off the trail, I crossed a small creek to take a break, eat a snack, and assess the storm.

With a snack in my stomach, most of the dark clouds had moved off to the south, though I could still hear thunder rumbling in the distance. I ventured back to the trail, still cautious as I headed toward a treeless area at 11,056 feet, with a metal bear canister in my backpack.

As I climbed onto Big Horn Plateau, it began to snow. The clouds obscured some of the panoramic views of the Sierra mountains, though I could still see much of the surrounding landscape. I was exhausted and cold, and I wasn’t particularly happy about being out there in the weather. But stopping wasn’t even a consideration.

My last obstacle for the day was Tyndall Creek. One of the trail notes mentioned a more manageable crossing about a half-mile upstream. As I started heading upstream, a hiker on the other side spotted me and came down to the creek.

I didn’t see him right away and couldn’t hear him over the rushing water. He pointed toward the spot where the trail crossed and told me to just cross there.

Again, I took off my shoes, socks, gaiters, and calf compression sleeves before stepping into the frigid water.

There were a few small groups of hikers scattered among the campsites. I found a semi-flat, semi-secluded spot and set up camp. I made dinner and got ready for bed. Tomorrow was going to be an early start. I was a little nervous about Forrester Pass. I had 5 miles to the top and didn’t want to be plunge-stepping down the north side in the afternoon. I was glad I wouldn’t have to cross Tyndall Creek in the morning.

At 3:00 a.m., I woke to bright stars in the cold morning sky. I mixed coffee into cold water and packed snacks into my pockets. After packing up camp, I took off my puffy and hit the trail at 4:40 a.m.

Hiking in the early morning light, I didn’t need my headlamp. The colors in the sky were stunning.

The trail crossed small snowmelt creeks, weaving in and out of stream crossings. At one point, the melting snow had created a stream right across the trail.

My feet crunched over the frozen ground, and I slipped a bit on a patch of ice. I realized I needed to be more careful crossing snow bridges, especially with rushing water underneath.

The only person I saw that morning was Scott, who passed me. I was still hoping to find a hiking buddy for the pass, and I was glad everything was so frozen.

As I continued climbing, I kick-stepped up increasingly steep icy fields, trying to avoid the boulders that were beginning to melt and weaken the snow in the morning sun.

As I looked up in awe at 13,897-foot Junction Peak next to it, Forrester Pass, at 13,120 feet, loomed ahead like a boss. I could see two people crossing over its scary icy chute above.

Just then, I heard someone hiking on the ice behind me. I was surprised to see Scott again. He had taken a break off trail, and I must have passed him at some point.

I told Scott we should hike together up and over the pass, and he agreed. I’m sure he did it just so I would feel more comfortable hiking it. Once we were out of the valley bowl, the switchbacks were mostly clear. They received afternoon sun each day.

As I made my way toward Forrester Pass, I felt a mix of intimidation and excitement. The climb ahead was daunting, and the icy terrain made me nervous, especially the chute. I was more worried about crossing the chute than the north side, although it would turn out that I probably should have been more concerned about the other side. Still, I felt confident in my ability to tackle the challenge. Reaching Forrester Pass would be a huge milestone, both physically and personally. It was the highest point on the PCT, and I was excited to see what it would take to get there.

When we finally reached the ice chute, I watched Scott scoot across the iced-over, foot-shaped holes in the ice. I questioned whether I should take the time to put on my microspikes. I hadn’t worn them yet on the trail.

My heart started to race. I secured my microspikes to the bottom of each shoe and exchanged one of my trekking poles for my ice axe. I crossed the ice chute one foot at a time, never looking down and maintaining three points of contact. Just like that, I was on the rocky trail on the other side.

Thrilled to be over the ice chute, I hollered down into the valley below. Just a few more switchbacks to the highest point on the PCT!

As I attempted to take a drink of my coffee, I discovered that the liquid around the lid had frozen, and there were chunks of ice inside the bottle. Thirsty as I was, I laughed. How often do you get a frozen coffee with a view like this?

As I crested the pinnacle of the PCT, Forrester Pass, I hollered at the top of my lungs in excitement. We stopped to take in the view and take photos on the blisteringly cold pass. Afterward, we decided it was too icy to glissade, so we would hike down instead. There was more snow on the north side because it received less sunlight during the early spring months. I was glad I had my microspikes. Scott didn’t have any.

Unable to see the trail, we followed footprints as we switchbacked down the ridge, eventually ending up farther out than we should have. Unaware that we were that far off trail, I told Scott I would probably be fine from there, and he picked up his pace.

Following the footprints, we ended up at the edge of the ridge. Scott climbed down over the snow and rocks and continued on. Dark, foggy clouds began rolling in. I hesitated, wondering whether I should backtrack alone to look for the trail or attempt to follow him. I was still reluctant to use my ice axe.

Just then, Scott slipped and slid, landing in a pile of rocks. He yelled out that he was hurt.

Thankfully, Yak and Gezelle were already below us and rushed over to help. I tossed them my first-aid kit.

I decided it was unsafe to try to climb down with my heavy, off-balance backpack, so I took it off and lowered it down using my bear line.

By the time I safely reached the bottom, Scott had already continued hiking on his own. The girls were packing up the first-aid kit and shouldering their packs as two young hikers who had just glissaded down the pass hiked toward us.

They had been hiking with the girls and were fast hikers. We were really concerned about Scott, even though he had an SOS button. The guys hiked ahead to try to catch up with him.

The three of us women continued hiking downhill, passing stunning teal-blue alpine lakes and crossing streams alongside the roaring Bubbs Creek. We were surrounded by some of the most epic views of beautiful white granite peaks.

At Mile 784, at an elevation of 10,961 feet, I took off my microspikes. Eventually, most of the snow was gone, replaced by puddles along the rocky trail and the occasional large snowdrift that I had to plunge-step through.

Below 10,000 feet and back in the trees, the girls stopped for a break while I continued hiking. When I reached Bubbs Creek, I noticed a downed tree upstream. I decided to take my shoes off and wade across instead. Waterfalls cascaded everywhere, and the creek was rushing with so much water. Along the trail, I also spotted a young buck.

two guys from earlier, who were taking a break on the side of the trail. They told me Scott was still alive and hadn’t bled out. This was where they had caught up to him, and he was still hiking at a good clip. I was happy to hear the news and relieved that he was doing okay.

With my early start, my quilt had some condensation on it when I packed up. I considered stopping to dry it out, but noticed a cooler breeze and clouds starting to roll in from the west. I wondered if we might get some precipitation.

I continued down the switchbacks toward Vidette Meadow. I was exhausted by the time I reached the trail junction, where there was a bear box. I found a spot a little farther off the trail and set up my tent around 12:30 p.m.

I collected water and filtered 1.75 liters. After making my Chicken and Vegetable Rice Mountain House meal and having cocoa coffee, I settled in for a nap from 1:00–2:30 p.m. While I napped, my body heat helped dry the condensation from my quilt.

After waking, I decided to stay at the lower elevation rather than climb higher because the weather was uncertain. I spent the afternoon blogging and relaxing at camp. I was almost out of food, and after eating a big lunch, I settled for some candy and made tea. Around 7 p.m., I fell asleep again.

At 11:30 p.m., I woke to the sound of precipitation hitting my tent. It was barely sleeting. I checked the tension on my tent, then curled back into my quilt.

In the morning, my tent was covered in a very thin layer of sleet that had slightly melted on contact and stuck to the tent. One guyline had slipped out of place, and a corner of the tent was sagging under the weight. The ground was dusted with tiny sleet pellets and snow.

Before putting on my shoes, I slipped a twist-tie snack bag over each foot. If my feet got wet in these temperatures, I might have trouble keeping them warm. I wanted to do everything I could to keep them dry.

Around 7:00 a.m., I left camp, making fresh tracks on the trail.

Not long after starting, I arrived at a wide stream feeding into the roaring Bubbs Creek. I picked my way across its multiple channels, stepping on fallen logs and large rocks while using my trekking poles for balance.

I arrived at a trail junction, turned right, and began climbing out of the canyon. As I gained elevation, the trees began to thin, and the terrain gave way to solid rock. As I switchbacked across the cliffs, I had a beautiful view of Kings Canyon and could see the valley I had hiked down the day before. Forrester Pass itself was hidden behind a mountain.

The Kearsarge Pinnacles, along with East and West Vidette Mountains, towered over the valley, dusted with snow. The scenery was so beautiful that it hardly looked real, with every detail highlighted by the white snow.

Crossing flowing streams and seasonal snowmelt, I was glad I had put the bags over my feet that morning. My shoes got damp, but my feet stayed dry and warm.

There was a brief relief from the climbing as I passed the first trail junction toward Bullfrog Lake and Kearsarge Pass. The plateau was flat and sandy, with a few scattered pine trees. I could see unfamiliar mountains towering in the distance and hear what sounded like military jets booming overhead, though I couldn’t see a single one.

I took the second trail junction, adding 7.6 bonus miles up and over Kearsarge Pass to the trailhead. As I climbed, the trees became increasingly sparse. Near the cutoff leading back down to Bullfrog Lake, I noticed a ranger’s note posted on a rock. It was titled “Bear Info” and warned hikers that bears in the area had gotten food from people and would continue trying to get more. It reminded hikers never to leave food unattended and to keep all food and scented items secured.

The note also included some rather unusual advice about what to do if you encountered a bear!

At 10:00 a.m., I reached Kearsarge Pass at 11,760 feet after hiking five miles and gaining 2,200 feet in elevation.

Inside the Sierras, the skies were partly cloudy with patches of blue. On the Highway 395 side, the sky was cloudy and gray, and I could see a storm blowing in from the north. I was relieved that I had beaten the storm to the pass.

I met a hiker named Crush who was hiking back over the pass. Just then, it started to lightly snow, and it continued throughout the rest of my hike. I was cold as I began the long switchbacks down toward Onion Valley. Big Pothole Lake was covered in ice, its surface a deep blue. On the other side of the lake, three people were practicing self-arresting with their ice axes on the snow.

Passing Gilbert Lake, I noticed patches of green grass and scattered trees around the partially frozen lake. The three men I’d seen practicing with their ice axes passed me on the trail. I was too shy to ask for a ride, thinking they would be long gone by the time I reached the trailhead.

As the trail continued downhill, I had views of waterfalls cascading down the steep valley walls. In the final mile, the parking lot came into view. I could still see the three men, so I picked up my pace, hoping they were still at the trailhead. If they were, I was going to ask for a ride.

When I reached the parking lot, I hurried over to where the three men were sitting in their vehicles with the windows down. Thankfully, they had been waiting for me!

Frank and his sons had permits for Mount Whitney on Friday, weather permitting, and had been practicing with their ice axes at elevation. They were heading to Lone Pine in their two vehicles and offered me a ride.

I stopped at the post office in Lone Pine to pick up my new base layer. Frank and his sons then dropped me a few blocks away at the gear store, where I repacked my backpack.

I was hoping to get a bunk at The Hostel California in Bishop. The hostel was first come, first served, and with cellphone service issues in the area since the windstorm back at Kennedy Meadows South, I wasn’t able to secure a spot ahead of time.

I hiked to the north edge of town and stuck out my thumb, hoping to catch a ride toward Bishop.

Someone pulled over, but they were only going as far as Independence. I thanked them but decided to hold out for a ride all the way to Bishop.

Mark, from Tehachapi, picked me up. He was heading past Bishop to retrieve his family’s RV at June Lake. His family had been camping there with their trailer when they decided to drive into Yosemite the previous day. It ended up snowing, and Tioga Road closed, leaving them unable to get back to their trailer. They had to drive home to Tehachapi, and Mark was now making the trip back to retrieve the RV.As we approached Bishop, I called The Hostel California to check for a bunk. Unfortunately, they were full. Not sure where to go that wouldn’t cost me over three bills, I asked Mark to drop me off at Smart & Final.

I picked up a bagged salad, a pint of ice cream, a gallon of milk, and a box of cereal for dinner and breakfast. Then I started walking down the snowy streets of Bishop.

Hotel prices were high because the Mule Days festival had just taken place. Heartbroken that the hostel was full and unsure whether I knew any other hikers in town, I started walking down the snowy main street, going from motel to motel asking for a hiker discount.

By my third stop, I was struggling to hold back tears. If only I knew someone in town I could split a room with. The snowy weather had left the streets nearly empty.

After leaving my fourth motel, I was considering stealth camping when an angel named Erine pulled over. She had seen me at the store earlier and noticed me wandering down the street. She was a local teacher.

She asked where I was headed. Not really sure myself, I explained that I was looking for a motel that might offer a discount for hikers.

When we arrived at the motel, Erine, the angel of a woman, paid for my room. She also made sure I got my backpack and groceries inside quickly because of the precipitation.

Before leaving, she gave me her phone number in case I needed anything while I was in Bishop and gave me a hug. Then she was gone.

After taking a shower and doing some laundry, I spiderwebbed my bear line around the room. There weren’t many good places to tie it off, but I managed to create a makeshift clothesline.

I spent some time blogging before eating half the salad for dinner. I also brushed the caps of my two Smartwater bottles, which I had been carrying since Mexico. It’s a chore I try to do in every town.

I fell asleep in a soft, warm bed with the TV on.

The skies had cleared up in town. You could still see fresh snow covering the mountains, with clouds beginning to move back over them. I spent my morning eating cereal, drinking coffee, and blogging. It felt good to have time to catch up without feeling so rushed and to have a little less stress on my shoulders.

I received a group message and found out that Google was in town staying at the Motel 6. He offered to split his bed with me for the night.

When checkout time came, I walked down the road with my backpack to the Motel 6. Google and two other hikers had been there for days, and their gear was scattered around the room. I threw my backpack in a corner away from everyone else’s gear.

There was a group wanting to go to Keough’s Hot Springs outside of town. I was unsure if I wanted to go, still having chores to do before leaving town. With snow still predicted in the mountains that day, I had no excuse not to go.

With no rideshares available, we started walking down the road and eventually found a hitch who even offered to take us the few miles off the main road to the hot springs.

Once there, we entered the bathhouse. There was a large, warm pool for swimming and a smaller, hot, shallow pool for soaking. The warm pool had no roof, offering a glimpse of the surrounding scenery. Dark storm clouds hung over the Sierra peaks, making being in the warm water feel even better.

After spending a few hours at the pool, most of it soaking in the hot water and enjoying a pretzel Google shared with me, the group walked the mile back to Highway 395 to hitch a ride to Bishop.

The storm had given the desert valley a beautiful, moody feel, with clouds draped over the White Mountains in front of us.

After returning to the motel with my resupply box, I set it aside to deal with tomorrow. I still hadn’t decided how many days of food I would need for the next stretch. With several challenging mountain passes ahead, I wanted to take some time to make sure I was prepared for what was coming. I spent the rest of the evening relaxing in the room while the others headed out for pub trivia and bowling. It was nice to have a quiet evening before heading back into the mountains.

#8203. Ukrainian Solos New Route in Gokyo Valley, Near Everest

Mykyta Balabanov of Ukraine has soloed a new route on the northeast face of Nepal’s Phari Lapcha (6,017m). Also known as Machhermo Peak, it sits 24km west of Everest in the Gokyo Valley.

Balabanov is still trekking back to Lukla, but has given ExplorersWeb some details and photos about this impressive new route.

The Ukrainian set off on his own up an unclimbed line on the northeast face on November 19. He made two bivouacs on the way up and one more on the descent.

“The level of difficulty is hard to tell, as always when solo, but I’d say it was around 80° on ice, M5+, 5c on rock, and lots of poor snow!” Balavanov said.

He estimates the new route is 1,200m long, and spans some 850 vertical meters from base to summit.

Rough topo of Balabanov’s solo route on the northeast face of Phari Lapcha. Photo: Mykyta Balabanov

Another side of Phari Lapcha saw action just one day before Balabanov, when U.S. guide Bennie Lieber led Canadian Emma Deguara up the Stone(d) Yeti route on the south face.

“I was not related to that climb, as I climbed alone after approaching from a different valley,” Balabanov explained. “However, I saw their tracks on the summit and spotted their bivy place at the bottom of the south face.”

Lieber and Deguara made the second ascent of their chosen route. “While the technical grade (M3, AI2+) is easily digestible, the room for mistakes, due to exposure, is zero,” Lieber said.

Mykyta Balabanov and fellow Ukrainian Mikhail Fomino opened a new route on Ama Dablam in 2024, but they first became widely known for their new route on the Southeast Ridge of Annapurna III in 2021.

#8200. ‘I Tried Not Looking at the Rack.’ Indiana Hunter Tags 199-Inch Buck on State Park Land

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 6:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Avery Fisher of Delphi, Indiana, and his best friend since high school, Nathan Van Dokkenburg, put in for an Indiana parks draw hunt on the recommendation of a buddy. Both drew the two-day Nov. 16-17 firearms season.

Indiana has special limited-draw, short-term deer hunts on about 25 public parks around the state. The hunts help manage the burgeoning deer herds, and most allow sportsmen to take three deer per hunt, with a limit of one buck. The number of hunters drawn for each park varies, but about 150 hunters are typically allowed on a park for each two-day hunt.

Neither Fisher nor Van Dokkenburg had ever walked the park they drew to hunt, which they declined to name. But they spent a lot of time figuring out where they should be on the property well before the hunt.

“We studied a lot of Google Maps and onX to get an idea where we should hunt far back in the park well away from other hunters,” Fisher tells Outdoor Life. “It’s rolling hills with big creek drains and draws … I’ve hunted that kind of land previously, and I had a good idea where to go early that Nov. 16 opening morning.”

The hunters checked in when the park opened at 5 a.m. and were prepared for an all day sit with packs, gear, and food.

“We got far back into the park, about one and a half miles from the road,” says Fisher, 25, who works as a financial advisor. “We stayed on ridgetops, and about one mile in, Nathan and I split up.

Fisher went about another half mile, hiking by the light of his headlamp.

“In the dark I spotted a tree line that had a lot of buck rubs. Then I saw a doe, and I figured it was a pretty good place.”

He climbed about 30 feet off the ground and settled into his saddle with his coffee and snacks his favorite .44-cal Ruger, a gift from his father years ago.

It was cold early that morning, and he heard something in a nearby ravine around 7:45 a.m.

“I thought I saw a buck and put my binoculars on it, and it was one with a good spread and nice tines about 100 yards away.”

He raised his rifle and rested it against the tree for support while looking for the buck in his scope.

“But my breath kept fogging the scope,” he says. “I kept cleaning it with my gloves, and the deer kept getting closer … I tried not looking at his rack as he turned uphill and then I saw his sticker points as he was rubbing trees.”

Fisher found the shoulder in his scope and squeezed the trigger. The buck ran uphill past Fisher, wobbling as it went. When the buck stopped at 60 yards, Fisher shot again. The buck dropped.

Fisher texted Van Dokkenburg and told him he’d shot a nice buck, but to keep hunting because they were allowed to shoot up to three deer apiece.

“A group of does came in at about 9 a.m., and I shot one and it fell,” Fisher says. “I decided not to shoot any more deer that morning.

“We had to bone and pack out two deer like we were on a Western hunt. I figured that was enough for us to lug out of there that day.”

Van Dokkenburg saw nothing to shoot and met up with Fisher about 11 a.m., filming Fisher as he first walked up to his buck and realized how huge it was.

“I was speechless at the size of his rack,” says Fisher. “I knew he was big, but not a rack like that. I’m grateful and thankful to the Lord.”

Using a wheeled cart and backpacks, the two hunters hauled both deer back down to their vehicle.

“I think my pack with deer and gear weighed over 100 pounds, and I had to stop and offload more from my pack to the wheeled cart,” Fisher says.

They then drove to check in their deer with park personnel.

Fisher’s 13-point buck has been measured with an unofficial green score of 199 5/8 inches. It turned out Fisher’s second shot had been unnecessary, since his first had struck the heart.

Read Next: ‘Complete Shock.’ Long-Haul Trucker Hunter Tags 227-Inch Nontypical During State Park Hunt

The two buddies returned to the park to hunt the next day, though Fisher says they were exhausted from the previous day’s hunt.

“We saw some deer, including another far-off buck, but we didn’t shoot another deer that second day,” he says. “Nathan is drawn for another two-day park hunt later this year, so he still has a good chance of taking deer, maybe even a huge buck.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8199. High Winds Thwart Latest Everest Attempt

The Russian team has returned to Everest, aiming to reach at least 7,500m, but high winds have again dashed their plans.

The three-man team of Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov set off yesterday and reached their cached tent at 5,600m. As leader Vasiliev explained, they expected strong winds at summit altitude until Wednesday, but they intended to keep themselves in lower areas, where the wind speed was acceptable.

However, the wind was too strong to go higher than 5,600m today, so they decided to stay put. MountainClub.com, which has access to the team’s InReach tracker, has posted a partial view of the climbers’ current location:

We asked Jost Kobusch of Germany (also planning to climb the West Ridge of Everest this winter) about that location. He confirmed that they are midway up the vertical mixed section leading from the Khumbu Glacier to the Lho La Pass.

This is the expedition’s fourth foray up the mountain. According to Vasiliev, it is also their last chance to acclimatize before a potential summit push. The Russians’ climbing permit is reportedly valid until December 15.

Their future progress will depend on the wind. Multi-model forecasts indicate that winds are expected to decrease on Thursday but not to disappear. The chart by High Asia Avalanche Watch, which compares seven different weather models, predicts that winds will not drop below 45kph at the summit (on Thursday and Friday). They will then pick up again.

Wind speed at the summit of Everest over the next two weeks, by High Asia Avalanche Watch.

#8197. 15 Scent Control Strategies for Deer Hunting That Actually Work

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 22, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

A heavy-antlered 12-point buck materializes on the trail. He’s only five steps from bow range, but then he throws his nose in the air, goes rigid with nervousness, and waves the white flag. Game over. That’s all, folks.

We all know that any successful deer hunt starts with not getting busted by a whitetail’s incredibly powerful sense of smell. And today there’s a whole collection of gear that’s marketed as being able to beat a deer’s nose. Many of the scent control gear and strategies are gimmicks, but some are not.

With careful scent control, you can give yourself a slight edge that just might mean the difference between getting busted and getting a shot. Here are scent-reduction efforts that work, with 15 steps that hunters can use to minimize scent before, during, and after the hunt.

Hunters must understand they will never completely neutralize the scent they emit. Furthermore, they will never completely beat a whitetail’s nose. However, a hunter can minimize their scent.

This won’t keep a deer from smelling you, but it might buy you a little extra time or a few more yards. It can also make all the difference in a non-target deer just moving along rather than alarm snorting.

You don’t have to use all of the following strategies just to shoot a deer. But, implementing at least some of them will certainly make you more effective in the stand.

Deer hunters should consider a long-term scent control mentality. Rather than just preparing the day of the hunt, start mitigating scent long before. During hunting season, consider cutting the smelly shampoos, soaps, colognes, deodorants, and aftershaves out of your routine. This will help keep your clothes smelling more natural, too.

Washing your hunting clothes is the next step. Treat with scent-reduction laundry detergent. Such laundry detergent doesn’t remove all scent, but it knocks out a lot of it and it doesn’t add unnatural scents. (Also, on a deer vision note, ensure that the detergent does not contain UV brighteners.)

Once your hunting clothes are clean, store them in a dedicated container. A portable plastic tote that has a place at home, but easily loads into a vehicle, is the best choice. This should help minimize odors that might contaminate the garment fibers. A lot of hunters like to put a pine branch in their tote to keep clothes smelling fresh and piney (and not like plastic).

In addition to hunting clothing, hunters should also clean their other gear. Backpacks and boots absorb the scents around them. So, store these and other hunting items in areas that aren’t stinky. Use a dedicated storage bin for this gear, too.

The day of the hunt, take a shower with shampoos and soaps that are designed to reduce human odor without adding artificial scents. This should help neutralize some (but not all) of the existing surface scent on your hair and skin.

Brush your teeth, but not with a super-smelly toothpaste. Instead, use something with a natural scent, or no scent at all. Then, eat an apple to help further mitigate scent that might originate from the mouth and throat. Avoid strong-smelling drinks and foods, such as coffee or spicy grub which lead to bad breath.

A lot of hunters use ozone in the field (like Ozonics), but it’s also applicable for treating hunting clothing. Some products are designed to work in conjunction with boxes to clean clothing and remove unwanted scent.

Used improperly, Ozone can destroy clothing. So Wildgame innovations recently introduced a ZeroTrace tote and device that is designed to treat gear without destroying it.

Read Next: Does Ozonics Actually Work? Here’s What 5 Independent, Diehard Deer Hunters Say After Field Testing

To minimize unwanted scent particles from making it onto your hunting clothes, consider dressing once in the field. This keeps away scent from handling pets, visiting gas stations, or other stops between home and the hunt. Getting dressed in the field during a cold or rainy day can be a hassle, but it’s worth it.

Rubber boots can contain some scent, but they only achieve this if kept uncontaminated from foreign scents. Don’t wear your deer hunting boots around town where they might pick up unwanted odors.

Hunters have many reasons for field wipes. But in terms of scent control, wiping off sweat with a field wipe will certainly help. Just make sure the version you use is scent-free.

Even better than wiping away sweat, is to not break a sweat at all. Sweat just adds an entire fresh new layer of scent. In the field, do everything possible to prevent or reduce sweating. Wait to put on your coat until you get to your stand.. Walk slowly. Remain in the shade. Take more time to get settled in the treestand.

Perhaps the most important aspect of scent control is managing the direction that your scent travels. This means choosing good entry and exit routes, and ensuring your wind doesn’t carry over into areas that deer frequent (especially bedding areas and core feeding areas).

Those who hope to minimize scent even further should deploy hunting blinds that are completely enclosed. Those which have completely sealed doors and windows can hold most, if not all, scent within the blind. Keeping windows closed until it’s  time to shoot is an excellent way to bottle up scent.

Some hunters might cleverly use wind blockers, which are land features that keep deer from getting downwind. Examples include hunting along the edge of a bluff, river, hinge-cut, or lake. Similarly, you can make a mock scrapes on either side of your scent cone, which might stop a buck just long enough before he gets downwind of you.

The rising and falling thermals can be helpful or a huge pain in the butt. When used wisely, and with the right stand locations, thermals will carry scent up and away from deer. For example, hunting up high on a ridge when morning thermals carry scent upward makes it very difficult for deer to smell hunters. Likewise, hunting down low in a bottom when afternoon thermals carry scent downward can achieve the same result.

Remember, no matter how far you take scent control strategies, deer will eventually smell you. It’s important to take all precautions, and implement each step outlined above. Ultimately, careful stand selection that puts the wind in your favor will be the most important step for not getting busted.

But don’t get discouraged when the inevitable happens. Just keep hunting. After all, you can’t really beat a deer’s nose, and it wouldn’t be fun if we won every time anyway.

#8193. German Women Climb New Route on Sharphu VI

Last month, five women with the German Alpine Club established a new route on the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI in eastern Nepal. It was only the second overall ascent of the 6,076m peak near Kangchenjunga.

In October, Steffi Feistl, Anna Gomeringer, Kristin Hinkelmann, Kathi Huber, and Fenja Kochl set up their base camp at 4,000m. A huge amount of snow had fallen earlier that season, guaranteeing arduous slogging in some areas.

Their preliminary plan had been to attempt either Sharphu II or Sharphu VI, but Sharphu II’s condition looked too dangerous due to unstable rock, threatening seracs, and hazardous snow conditions.

Sharphu II and VI. Photo: Xtreme Climbers/labels by Gunter Seyfferth

On the other hand, the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI seemed feasible. The team acclimatized over several reconnaissance trips, overnighting up to 4,800m. They then set up their advanced base camp on a moraine ridge at that altitude. Then Kochl, Hinkelmann, and Huber set off over initially easy but deep snow-covered terrain.

They eventually reached a steep section at around 5,300m where powder snow overlying bare rock made placing protection difficult. “The mixed pitches had looked easy, but were steeper than expected,” recalled Huber. “Nasty, granular snow, and no hold on the rock underneath.”

Their work complete, the trio rappelled down, and the following day, Feistl and Gomeringer took their turn. They inched up a difficult mixed pitch followed by 200 vertical meters of burrowing through waist-deep powder.

The route up the northwest ridge of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

After a rest day, the five women set off for the summit. They reached a glacier plateau via a 5,800m, previously unclimbed sub-peak. Here, they bivvied. On October 21, they began the final two kilometers to the main summit. The recent sun had hardened the snow, so they sank at most knee-deep. Eventually, they reached the summit ridge. They had to take turns standing on the summit, because that final ridge was so narrow.

Gomeringer and Kochl on the summit of Sharphu VI. Photo: German Alpine Club

Sharphu VI was first climbed on March 25, 2025, by a Chilean-Nepalese team who thought they’d climbed a different mountain. They went up via the east flank. Before that, Sharphu VI had been attempted only once, in 2023.

The German women’s route featured 1,300m of elevation gain, graded M5, 55°. The expedition was the culmination of the German Alpine Club’s three-year women’s training program.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8191. ‘No Dad, This Is Your Deer.’ Hunter Gets a Second Chance at a 200-Inch Blackpowder Buck

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 22, 2026 2:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

During Virginia’s muzzleloader season last year, Mike Shifflett got a chance at a giant buck.

“I was worried I might have hit him,” Shifflett tells Outdoor Life. “But we looked hard and found no evidence he was hit. I sat in a blind later looking for him and eventually spotted him on trail cameras and he was fine. I completely missed him.”

Virginia had a very harsh winter last year, says the 50-year-old farm manager from Crozet, and he was concerned the buck wouldn’t survive. But he started seeing the whitetail again on trail cameras — then found its sheds.

“I’m sure glad I missed him, because when I started getting trail camera photos of him this summer, his rack had just exploded. His antlers had added at least 30 inches.”

Shifflett monitored the buck through summer and early autumn on trail cameras in the rolling green hills of northern Virginia. He concluded the deer was very much a loner.

“He wasn’t aggressive at all,” Shifflett explained. “He completely avoided other bucks. If there was another buck in a field edge I hunted, he’d never come out.”

He started hunting the deer during this blackpowder season, which opened Oct. 31 and is Shifflett’s favorite time to chase whitetails.

He got trail camera photos of the buck and saw him once, far away at dusk.

On Nov. 6, Shifflett waited for his 16-year-old daughter, Chloe, to get home from school, then they rushed to their blind. The hardsided redneck sat  on a field edge near the top of a mountain, with deer trails feeding into the field.

A little after 5 p.m., the buck stepped out at 200 yards. There were some does in the field, and he began working toward them.

“He worked a scrape and kept walking a few steps at a time toward the does. He was locked on those does, but it took him at least 15 minutes to get to them.

Then the buck, now at 90 yards, started pushing one doe around.

“He was in range and I offered my gun to my daughter to shoot him, but she said ‘No Dad, this is your deer.’”

Shifflett raised his scoped .50-cal T/C and fired.

“The air filled with smoke when I shot, and when it cleared the buck had its tail up, and went into the woods. It was surreal, like he wasn’t hit at all.”

But Chloe had filmed her dad shooting the buck, and the video showed the deer mule kick at the shot.

“But I still didn’t like the way the deer acted as it left the field and into the woods,” says Shifflett.

The two quietly climbed out of the blind and left, planning to return the next morning to track the buck. They returned early the following day with Shifflett’s wife, his son, and a few other helpers.

The search party didn’t initially locate much of a blood trail. But 40 yards into the woods the blood became easy to see. Soon they found the buck dead about 100 yards from the hit site. Shifflet’s bullet had passed through both lungs.

“There was a respectful, silent reverence among us — almost like being in a church.”

Once they got the buck home, Shifflett and his buddies measured its rack.

“I’m no trophy hunter, and when we started scoring him at home I realized it was a really big buck,” he says. “I had entered a local big buck contest earlier this year and they scored my deer at 194 inches. It’s leading the contest with a $1,000 gift card as top prize.”

He got in touch with Virginia Buckmasters scorer Tyler Turner, who measured the 16-point whitetail slightly bigger (Buckmasters has a different scoring system than some traditional record keeping organizations, including Boone and Crockett and Pope and Young). His spread was 19.6 inches and taped out at 202 ⅛ inches.

“Tyler says it’s the biggest Virginia buck in their ‘Regular Deer’ category that’s ever been taken by bow, blackpowder, or gun. He also told me it’s the ninth largest Buckmasters deer in that rack category nationwide.”

It’s been a whirlwind of congratulations since news of his great buck spread. But one thing he says he’ll always remember is when he and his family walked up to the buck that morning.

Read Next: ‘I Tried Not Looking at the Rack.’ Indiana Hunter Tags 199-Inch Buck on State Park Land

“When we found him, everyone became real quiet,” he says. “There was a respectful, silent reverence among us – almost like being in a church.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8190. Piolet d'Or Climber Bags New Route on Kangchung Shar

Hiroyoshi Manome of Japan and his partners, Koji Shiogai and Subaru Takeda, are back in base camp after climbing a new route via the southwest pillar on Kangchung Shar in the Everest region.

The three climbed the line in four days and three nights from their base in Gokyo, Manome’s home team told ExplorersWeb. They summited in good weather and clear skies yesterday at noon. “The route was particularly interesting, with substantial technical rock climbing,” they explained.

The team believes it is the first ascent of this particular line and, as far as we know, they are right. The Himalayan Database has no record of any previous ascent of the southwest pillar.

Manome during the climb. Photo: Subaru Takeda

Kangchung Shar, located in the Gokyo Valley, has an altitude that varies between 6,030m to 6,103m, depending on the source. The Japanese team has shown that the peak still has plenty of adventurous terrain.

Manome explained before departure that their primary goal was the formidable 1,000m mixed north face, which has been climbed only once, with the east ridge as a backup.

“It will be a technical climb, which suits me just right, and there is little information available, so I think it will be an adventurous climb,” he said. The climber illustrated his plans with the hard-drawn sketch below. However, in the end, the climbers found a third option.

The Japanese team’s two potential routes. Photo: Hiroyoshi Manome

Kangchung Shar was first climbed in 1984 by Franci Knez of Slovenia, on a solo up the northwest ridge from Kangchung La. The 1,000m north face of Kangchung Shar was first climbed by  Zdenek Hak and Jaroslaw Bansky of the Czech Republic on their third try in 2021. No one has repeated it. After summiting, the Czech pair traversed to the south side for their descent.

Another Japanese team climbed neighboring Kangchung Nup (6,090m) in the spring of 2022. The American Alpine Journal describes Kangchung Shar and Kangshung Nup as “a bit like two Matterhorns side by side but two kilometers higher.”

An alpinist for 30 years, Hiroyoshi Manome focuses on technical, alpine-style climbs of 6,000m Himalayan peaks. He received a Piolet d’Or for the first ascent of the 2,200m south pillar of Kyashar (6,770m) in Nepal, with Tatsuya Aoki and Yasuhiro Hanatani. His latest big climb was another first ascent in November 2024, this time of the northeast ridge of Nepal’s Chhopa Bamare (6,109m), with Akitugu Konishi and Kotaro Kunimune.

Hiroyoshi Manome. Photo: The North Face Japan

#8179. ‘Get Out of the F—ing Tree!’ The Police Raided Our Deer Hunt

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 10:54 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

WHAT STARTED as a normal morning in the deer woods turned out to be the scariest day of my life. It was March 2020, and my friend Billy Phillips and I were hunting one of the suburban properties we have permission on in northern Virginia.

Our state has an extended urban antlerless season that ends in late March, and this particular hunt occurred right after the state of Virginia announced the “Stay at Home” order during the early Covid-19 pandemic. That particular order allowed you to leave home for a couple of reasons, and hunting was one of them.

We were hunting in a neighborhood of multimillion-dollar homes on three- to five-acre lots, and the property we were on that day was a double lot of 10 acres total. In our world, that’s humongous.

We parked at the end of an old driveway and walked a short way to our hunting spots. We climbed into our trees to set up about 100 yards from each other, with Billy closer to the trucks than my stand. Then, just as daylight broke, I spotted the lights of a car near where we parked. I looked through my binos and saw that an older Ford Taurus had pulled up. I remember thinking, Oh, this is kind of odd.

“Yeah, I’m looking at them,” he texted back.

Then I saw Billy’s truck bed light turn on.

“He’s in my car,” Billy texted again. “I’m getting down.”

At this point the sun was fully up and we could clearly see our surroundings. As Billy descended his tree, I saw a police officer in full uniform coming down the little hill toward us with an M4 in his hands. I pulled out my phone to record footage, the kind of video that says, Look at the unusual things we deal with while hunting in the suburbs.

Then I realized the cop had his weapon shouldered and was pointing it at Billy. He was screaming at him.

“Get on the ground! Get on the fucking ground!”

What he was saying was concerning, but the tension in the officer’s voice was worse. Billy was packing up his sticks as the cop approached. If Billy had turned around while holding the sticks or if he still had his crossbow in his hands, I don’t even want to think about what might have happened.

“Where’s your buddy?” the cop started yelling, getting even more aggressive. “Where’s your buddy?”

“Get out of the fucking tree,” he yelled.

“OK,” I replied, “it’s going to take me a minute.”

“I didn’t ask for feedback. I told you to get down here.”

The cop screamed at both of us the whole time it took me to get down. When I walked up to the cop, he spoke into his radio.

We were obviously perplexed, and he explained that he had snipers in the woods with their rifles trained on us.

“What is going on here?” I asked. “What is happening?”

“Shut up,” he told me. “I’m asking questions here. Hands on your head and turn around.”

I kept persisting with questions, trying to figure out why we were being searched at gunpoint and who this police officer was. The man wouldn’t tell us anything and proceeded to pat us down.

“I’m not a regular cop,” was all he said. “I’m a special cop.”

Read Next: The Best Tree Saddles, Tested and Reviewed

When the officer asked us what we were doing at this property, we explained that we are deer hunters and that we had permission to be here. I pulled up the signed permission slip from the landowner and showed it to him. Then I asked again what he was doing here. The officer told us that they spotted us on a UAV — an unmanned aerial vehicle. He was suggesting he had a military drone 30,000 feet above us, which is completely ridiculous for our county or most any police department.

“This is Fairfax County,” I said. “What are you talking about?”

His demeanor shifted and he got less aggressive — especially when I asked him why he was going through our vehicles.

“You had a wire on the floor, and I thought it was a bomb,” he said to Billy. Billy explained that he works in landscaping, and that he has sprinkler-head components in his truck.

By now we were starting to get nervous, and I noticed the officer’s uniform didn’t look quite right. There was something off about him. We kept asking why he was searching our cars without cause, and why he was on private property.

He didn’t give us his card or an explanation, and no other officers showed up. After the adrenaline of having a gun pointed at us wore off, Billy and I ran through what had just happened.

I told Billy that I thought the guy must be impersonating law enforcement, because the whole interaction just didn’t sit right with me. Billy and I mentor several police officers who are learning to hunt, and we’re huge supporters of police in general. Mind you, this was when Covid lockdowns were at their peak, and there was mass civil unrest throughout the country. The last thing we wanted to do was blast the police.

Still, we needed answers. We sent the short video clip I had recorded to a few of our police officer buddies, and one called me right away. He explained that it didn’t look right, and that what happened is not standard police procedure. He asked where we were and made a few phone calls to see if he could find out any details for us.

When my buddy called me back, he was laughing so hard he could barely speak. The man who had just ordered us down from our trees at gunpoint was indeed a police officer known as the department’s Tackleberry. (In the Police Academy movies, Tackleberry is a tryhard who’s overzealous in his enforcement.) This particular police officer had been on desk duty and was sent out on patrol as a result of scheduling issues caused by Covid.

My buddy learned that the officer had gotten turned around, thought he was at a park, and that we were doing something illegal. Billy and I had a meeting with the guy’s captain, who apologized profusely. We decided to leave it at that.

Read Next: Deer Hunting Every Day in the Suburbs of Virginia

It was a terrifying and confusing ordeal while it was happening, but now that a few years have passed, it’s just another crazy story from our time hunting the D.C. suburbs. Here’s this guy who thought he was busting the biggest crime ring of all time and instead ended up nearly arresting a couple rednecks in camo.

#8174. I Hunted Ducks and Geese During the Glory Days of Earthquake Lake

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 12:25 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

It was a good day for waterfowl hunters. Wisps of fog seeped out of the melting landscape, and the sky was so low and solid that the geese—if they came—would be close to the deck. And it was cold. Two days before, a savage cold wave had swept across Tennessee, plastering trees and highways with ice and freezing ponds thicker than anyone could remember. Ducks and geese had been sitting it out, but on this third day, the ice was melting, and they would fly.

I slid the overhead hatch back and stuck my head up and out of the blind. Earlier it had been too dark to see how we were situated. I found that we were in a soybean field of perhaps 300 acres, already plowed for the spring planting. There was sparse timber on all sides. To the south, the way hunters faced when standing in the blind, the timber was not so tall or thick, but there was the heavy undergrowth that grows at water’s edge. The lake is closer than I thought, I said to myself. It’s only 100 yards or so away.

The blind was one of the biggest I had ever seen, and it was also almost invisible. From a duck’s overhead view, it no doubt looked like a rush-covered finger of land surrounded on three sides by timber, water, and waterfowl decoys—hundreds of them.

Thus oriented, I closed the camouflaged hatch and placed my feet near the gas heater. Sheer luxury! Being there almost made up for all those miserable days I have spent crouched in freezing mud holes. Perhaps a time or two before, in Texas and Louisiana, I’d been in more luxurious shooting stands, but they were small houses built on stilts above the water. This was a blind dug into the ground, lined with concrete, and covered with earth that had been planted with suitable cover. The blind’s eyes on the outside world were 12 sliding hatches.

Inside, the padded theater seats were as comfy as easychairs. Theater seats! I thought. Who in hell would have thought of putting theater seats in a duck blind? But it was perfect! The spring-loaded bottoms snapped up out of the way when you rose to shoot. That gave a man more foot and leg room and eliminated bruised shins if he turned around for backyard shots. I’m forever scraping my shins on the sharp-edged board benches found in most blinds.

As the light increased, so did the yipping of the Canada geese rafted up on Lake Isom just beyond the brushy boundary. A few minutes before, the even din of honking had changed pitch when a raft of Canadas took wing. Moments later another flight heaved into the morning fog.

Our lookout Jerry Jones made no sign that geese were coming our way, but the atmosphere inside the blind was becoming so charged with excitement that you could almost touch it. Feet shuffled, experienced hunters quaked with anticipation, and hands rattled shotshells in jacket pockets to make sure fast reloads were ready. Overhead and close by, there was a whistle of teal. Jerry’s eyes followed their crossing flight, but he did not signal us to shoot, because he didn’t want to spoil our chances for the big honkers that could be only minutes away.

From the lake came another din of geese taking off, and Jerry pulled his head lower and tightened his grip on his Browning autoloading magnum. Then he crouched even lower, eyes level with his lookout hole, and said, “They’re ours boys. Get ready—now!”

Shotgun barrels poked out of the blind, and there was ragged fire at eight or nine geese that were angling by to our left. The closest bird went limp in midair, but the one I fired at twice merely did the double wobble of a wise old goose that knows he’s being shot at.

Long ago I learned that in each flight of waterfowl there are one or two obvious birds. They are closer or bigger or out in front, and they tend to draw fire. When I’m sharing a blind with other hunters, I therefore pass up the obvious targets and go for a more obscure member of the flight. That bird is all mine, and I know for sure if I hit or miss. This time the obvious bird did draw most of the fire and was the only one to fall. Happily there was no chorus of “I got him!” that sometimes mars such events. These men, most of whom I’d met only that morning, were too experienced and too polite for that. Instead, each one claimed to have missed by several feet and that the goose must have been felled by the youngest member of the party, 15-year-old Charlie Lawrence.

“I did?” he whooped in wide-eyed amazement. “And that means you’ll have to fetch it,” Charlie’s father almost managed to say before the young hunter was out of the blind and sprinting after the prize, his rubber boots going flub-flub and launching dollops of river-bottom mud from the Mississippi in all directions.

Whoever first said that you can tell a lot about a man’s character by hunting with him really knew what he was talking about. Settling back into my posh seat, I knew I had learned a lot about my new acquaintances already and had a feeling that we would all be good friends before the day ended.

The only one of us who hadn’t pulled a trigger on the geese was Ralph McDonald, the well-known wildlife artist. His sliding hatch had stuck when the action started, so he had to sit out the first round. But Jerry Jones fixes that in a hurry by leaving the hatch open and covering the opening with a layer of cane that could easily be pushed aside. I liked that arrangement better than moving the hatch, so he fixed me up the same way. It gave me a better look at incoming birds.

In addition to Jerry, Ralph, and myself, our shooting party included H. Lea Lawrence, his teen-aged sons David and Charlie, country-music star Jim Ed Brown, plus Bill Morrison and Tom Callery. Lea, who lives in Nashville, is an outdoor writer whose name is familiar to readers of OUTDOOR LIFE. Morrison and Callery are field and legislative assistants to U.S. Senator William Brock of Tennessee. When I met Morrison and Callery that morning and learned of their political connections, I made a mental note to bend their ears about pending firearms legislation.

But there was no time for that now—ducks were coming, and Jerry was making seductive sounds on his big-barreled duck call. I couldn’t see the incomers from my angle, but it was easy enough to pinpoint their position by watching Jerry’s eyes. As they came closer, he crouched lower in the blind, and I knew they were within range even before he whisper-shouted, “Now!”

About 10 spoonbills were winging low and fast from right to left in front of the blind. I swung my 12-gauge double, a Model 21 Winchester, through one of the trailers, but before I could press the trigger the bird’s head kicked back and he collapsed in midair. Then I was aware of three or four other ducks tumbling out of the formation, but my attention was concentrated on another spoonbill. I swung the barrels ahead by about two feet and pressed the trigger. The load of No. 4s folded the spoonbill so cleanly that there was no need to think about a follow-up shot, so I chased the barrels to the next closest bird and gave him the remaining round. It was a straightaway shot, and the range was rapidly increasing with each beat of his wings. For a moment it looked like a miss, but then the spoonbill did a turnover and went into a power dive before thumping hard on the semifrozen field. At 60 yards, perhaps more, it had been a lucky shot, but all eyes had been on me, and the welfare of my ego was at stake.

While David and Charlie fetched the downed waterfowl, I made the innocent mistake of asking Ralph if he had scored on the spoonbills. Though he is a leading wildlife artist whose paintings have twice been featured on the cover of the Ducks Unlimited magazine, he has a well-earned reputation as a hard-luck waterfowl hunter. On his goose hunts something always goes wrong, and he was still looking to shoot his first honker.

Jerry, who owns and operates Sunset Court and Dock on Reelfoot Lake, is one of those old-fashioned guides who believes that a day not spent hunting or fishing is a day wasted. Just to make sure that he and his customers get in plenty of both, his fees for a guided goose-and-duck hunt—including lodging, a heated blind, decoys, transport to the blind, plus some of the finest duck calling and yarn spinning you’re likely to hear anywhere—are no more than you’d expect to pay to spend the night in a rundown New York hotel.

While I was hunting the Reelfoot and Lake Isom area, the local waterfowl population was estimated at some 70,000 geese and about a quarter of a million ducks, mainly gadwalls and mallards.

Historic Reelfoot Lake is well known among waterfowlers and it is an easy day’s drive from many large Midwestern and Eastern cities. Lake Isom, however, remains distinctly undiscovered, even though it is only a few miles away (see map, page 89).

Hunting seasons run from December into January. The duck limit is based on the point system. Some ducks, such as pintails and spoonbills (shovelers), are rated at only 10 points each, so it is possible to bag 10 ducks plus two geese in a single day. That’s a lot of shooting.

Ralph was about halfway through a lengthy explanation of how he had failed to get off a single shot at the geese when Jerry shouted “Get down!” at the two young Lawrence brothers, who were still gathering ducks. Another flight of spoonbills had just cleared Isom’s boundary and were coming directly at us. Charlie had left his shotgun in the blind, but older and wiser David had his pump ready and neatly dropped a drake.

The first half-hour had produced a goose and a half a dozen ducks—rich promise of a good day of shooting—but by midmorning the action tapered off to only an occasional shot at a passing duck. For some reason known only to themselves, the geese had decided to leave by the western side of the lake. We could hear their comings and goings, but none came within half a mile. Over a breakfast of ham and eggs cooked in a subterranean kitchen attached to the blind and complete with gas stove, we discussed the sagging action. Jerry allowed that we could move to a blind closer to the flyway the honkers had selected that day. Jerry owns or has access to 15 blinds.

After a quick scouting sortie, he asked for volunteers to try another blind situated where the honkers seemed to be heading, but he also told us the road was bad and we’d have to walk a ways. Jim Ed Brown, Lea Lawrence, and I volunteered, but not without some misgivings on my part.

Ralph McDonald elected to stay where he was, because, as he put it, he’d “… had a vision that the geese would soon come that way.”

Even before we got to our new blind, there was no doubt that Jerry was right. While walking about a quarter of a mile through a sparse stand of scrub timber, we spotted three flights of geese skimming low over a field of winter wheat. They landed almost in the new blind.

“Holy catfish!” said Jim Ed in the soft Southern accents that have made him a favorite of Grand Ole Opry fans. “All I need is two.”

Just then six or eight more honkers slipped in low over the wheatfield, wings cupped to land. Before they touched down, the leader thought better of it, and they flared hard left, leveled off treetop high, and headed straight at us. We were almost through the scrub brush by then, and there wasn’t much cover, but we ducked into what concealment there was.

“Get ready,” Jerry whispered just an arm’s length to my left. Like me, he was half hidden in a honeysuckle bank. Just then Jim Ed and Lea opened up, and two honkers dropped at once. A third, hit hard, scaled overhead, and Jerry or me, or both of us, hit him at less than 30 yards and folded him cleanly.

The rest of the formation had swerved violently when Lea and Jim Ed started shooting, and they were out of range before I could come to bear with my second barrel. The geese were still reasonably close to Jim Ed, and with the last shot in his Model 12 Winchester pump, another Canada pitched down, wing feathers humming in the rush of the fall.

“Friends,” drawled Jim Ed, “this is what I was brought up to call good goose hunting. I’ve got my limit, and we ain’t even in the blind.”

The timing was perfect, because less than an hour later Jim Ed was scheduled to leave by charter plane to tape his popular “Nashville On The Road” TV show. So he and Jerry took the four honkers back to the truck while Lea and I went on to the blind. Jerry got back as quickly as he could.

We had no more than settled ourselves when a flight of perhaps 20 geese came soaring up the field. Jerry called, and they began to circle. When we stood to fire, they flared sharply and momentarily gave us that near-motionless target goose hunters love. I shot at a bird on the far right end of the formation so there would be little chance that Lea and I would double on the same target. The Canada spun and fell without a flutter.

Meanwhile Lea was doing the same with a honker he had cut out of the flight.

That evening we feasted on lake-country froglegs, catfish, hush puppies, and tart cole slaw. It had been a great day for everyone except Ralph McDonald.

“I was sure my hunch about those geese was a good one,” he lamented, pouring himself a stiff snort of Reelfoot tea, “but I never got a shot. My record is intact.”

Read Next: I Gave Up My Duck Blind to Hunt Where the Mallards Actually Are

Reelfoot Lake in far western Tennessee was formed by the New Madrid earthquakes of 1811-12. The catastrophe depressed some 50,000 acres that were later submerged by water from the Mississippi River. The surge was so great that the Mississippi is said to have flowed backward for a while. Due to siltation, plant succession, and drainage, Reelfoot Lake now covers about 18,000 acres.

A colorful legend accounts for the lake’s name. A Chickasaw chief suffered from a deformed or club foot and reeled when he walked. Chief Reelfoot could not persuade any of the Chickasaw maidens to become his wife, so he and his warriors kidnapped a Choctaw maiden named Starlight. Her father, who would not allow the marriage because of Reelfoot’s deformity, was so angered that he ordered his medicine men to bring down a terrible vengeance on Reelfoot and his people. That, the legend has it, was the cause of the earthquake.

Lake Isom, scene of the waterfowl hunt in this story, was isolated from the big lake when a road was built around the southern end of the lake. A water-control structure and a ditch aided in draining the land that lies between the lakes today. The lake was given a name when the site was acquired for a waterfowl refuge from the Isom family.

Reelfoot’s excellent waterfowl habitat made it one of the most famous hunting areas on the Mississippi Flyway. The open-water portions attracted a variety of diving ducks, particularly canvasbacks. Mallards were most abundant and most popular among gunners. Eventually, however, the canvasbacks almost disappeared, and restrictive limits were imposed on mallard shooting. In the late 1960’s, with a one-mallard limit, Reelfoot had an all-time low in hunter usage.

As the mallards and some other ducks increased in numbers after the low, and limits were increased, the popularity of Reelfoot Lake among gunners increased somewhat, but it is taking a while for hunters to realize that it’s the same hotspot. Reelfoot is as good today as it ever was, and Lake Isom is just as good. Neither Reelfoot nor Isom is managed by the state, but public hunting is permitted at Reelfoot. At Lake Isom the hunting is on private land.

#8171. From Gangs to Grizzlies

How a former gang member found a different kind of vigilance in the Montana wild.

One summer evening, I was hiking a trail near Marias Pass in Glacier National Park, returning to the car after a few hours of wildlife photography at a lake— nothing but deer and mosquitoes to show for it—when I noticed something blondish-brown moving toward me. It barely registered. I felt relaxed, though, truthfully, I’m never fully relaxed.

A grizzly cub, big as an overgrown Saint Bernard. I was fascinated—until reality hit. The mother wouldn’t be far behind.

“Hey, bear,” I shouted, my voice cracking. The cub woofed an alarm and took off to my left, a dark blur swallowed by the forest.

I turned toward the trail ahead. And—boom—there she was. Running at me, big and fast and flat to the ground.
